keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | AI platform
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as OpenAI Opens Its Platform
deck: Google's new model draws instant praise while OpenAI hands builders MCP servers, apps, and subscriptions. Meanwhile, a quieter shift: agents that act on their own, and the humans learning to trust them.
quote: I trust my dot to go have good judgment about what I need to think about, not think about. If there's something very urgent, otherwise handle things for me.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today is about the platform war going vertical. Google shipped Gemini 4 and builders immediately called it a win, while OpenAI turned ChatGPT into a place where anyone can build and deploy MCP servers, bring their subscription into third-party apps, and let agents run in the background. The deeper thread underneath the launches: everyone is now arguing about the deployment layer, not the model layer, because getting AI into real workflows is turning out to be the hard part.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it to three words: "Gemini 4 Argon!" The VP at Google, Google Labs, Gemini App, and Google AI Studio let the launch speak for itself.

**Peter Yang** was blunt about the reception: "Google cooked on Gemini 4!" He added that the remaining gap is on the harness side, naming **Antigravity** for coding and **Spark** for a personal agent as the places Google still needs to get more competitive.

**Thibault Sottiaux** had a packed day at OpenAI. He announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through ChatGPT, with access restricted to anyone you choose or shared with the world. He also noted that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model "pretty much ever" across both the API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed. He closed with a lighter note: he asked his dot to paint him, it went cartoonish, he told it to try harder and find a recent photo of him online, and it did better.

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, described an "absurdly high bias towards action": a casual feature discussion ends with a PR waiting at your desk, and a Slack lunch suggestion turns into a calendar invite within five minutes. He admitted the hair-trigger pace has downsides, but argued you cannot teach that energy and it is nearly impossible to get back once lost.

**Guillermo Rauch** made the case that **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing real usage from 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He said they turn down "free token" promos from companies with dubious **Zero Data Retention** claims, and noted most providers suck, so you want the best ones. Separately he pushed **Connect** as the fix for agents plagued by static credentials, framing it as both easier and more secure for the 20M+ developers and billions of agents they will ship.

**Aaron Levie** argued the big opportunity is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He listed what actually has to happen: legacy systems moved to the cloud, data access updated, software connected to agents, workflows reengineered, **HITL** figured out, evals generated and maintained, all continuously updated as new models ship. His punchline: AI is not the same as deploying software, because you are delivering work output inside a process, which opens up new kinds of firms. "Great time to be an FDE or FDE firm."

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described controllers at a command center sitting in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them, and airlines scheduling 32 planes into a 15-plane window. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which Duffy said competed with the biggest companies and won, predicting that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Duffy's answer to critics: "it's dumb AI, not smart AI," with controllers still making every call. Tan's takeaway: none of this needs a breakthrough, just the will to use software.

**Nikunj Kothari** automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking founders is "What is the maximalist version of this company?" His paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** is shutting down on Nov 17, 2026, with its learnings living on in the global launch of **skills** in Gemini App, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat.

**Amjad Masad** noted you can now make and one-click publish **Meta VR** apps.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git and GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders on complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also celebrated landing on a top podcast list among industry legends, beating MKBHD, without optimizing for Apple Podcasts.

**Thariq** shared work-in-progress notes on a game prototype, saying the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet, and that a real version would need 3-4 variations of the character and 8-10 characters total.

**Matt Turck** posted a chart on SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Peter Steinberger** changed the visibility of inter-agent communication in his harness to a single expandable line, calling the chat-stream noise increasingly irritating. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, and that it is time to rethink how we work.

**Dan Shipper** said he is ready to vibe check the new model and asked Sundar Pichai for access.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway: Sam Altman says the Dots gave him back his mornings, and that is the whole point of agents.**

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about the 22 things OpenAI launched, the difference between an industrial revolution and a renaissance, and what it actually feels like to hand your mornings to an agent.

Altman's framing is that people want so much more stuff, and that is a good thing. Developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either. He was never as doomery about jobs as others, but he has been surprised by how much latent demand existed and how much people remain at the center of everything. His example: 10,000 agents worked for four days in perfect coordination to solve a problem, and almost none of the public reaction was about the problem being solved. "We're obsessed with other people," he said. "We want to know about the person behind the art or the person behind the company."

On the platform question, Altman split the field into two schools: those who believe there will only be one company in the world, and those like OpenAI who believe in a renaissance of many companies, one-person companies, small teams building incredible things. He admitted they have not figured out the App Store equivalent, but argued they have figured out how to help millions build significant new products.

The most personal part was about **Dottie**, his dot. He used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters that got worse if ignored for an hour, and checking his phone polluted his brain. Now he trusts the dot to judge what needs his attention. He described a feature he was excited about but could not explain in enough detail to get built, so he jotted notes to his dot on walks, and it built five or six versions he could iterate on. Another time he spent 10 or 20 minutes searching Slack via Codex for something he was sure he had seen, gave up, and the next day his dot told him it had kept thinking about it overnight and found it: a screenshot, not text, which he had forgotten. "I would have given up on that."

On **Space**, he said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together, and that live documents which update automatically as people interact are something we do not have a word for yet. His own live dashboard is a changing summary of the business built just for him, based on what he cares about that week, which shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security now.

He also touched on the **Decisions API**, crediting Diogo, a former OpenAI colleague, for pushing the idea, and the number one request on Luna: cheaper and faster. On Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription into third-party apps, he said he hates signing up for 100 subscriptions and that your subscription should work wherever you want. His biggest excitement is for the apps nobody at OpenAI can imagine yet: "there are way more good ideas outside of our building than inside."

Aaron Levie, CEO of Box, sees a huge opportunity as the deployment layer for AI into the economy. Legacy systems need cloud migration, data access updates, agent connections, workflow reengineering, HITL design, and continuous evals. AI is not the same as deploying software: you are delivering actual work augmentation, which opens new kinds of firms. Great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

Garry Tan, President and CEO of Y Combinator, highlighted **Capy** for enabling agent swarms that speak to one another as peers and self-coordinate, allowing organic exploration instead of pre-arrangement. He also shared Secretary Duffy's example of **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence predicting air traffic, where a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes, noting none of this needs a breakthrough, just the will to use software.

Matt Turck, VC at FirstMark Capital, noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Nikunj Kothari, partner at FPV Ventures, has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part.

Peter Steinberger, building OpenClaw, finds inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line. He also noted the fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down.

Dan Shipper, CEO of Every, is ready to vibe check the new model and shared a piece on Every.

Aditya Agarwal, General Partner at SPC, asks everyone what the maximalist version of their company is. In AI, building is easier but the floor on what it means to be a great startup is dramatically raised.

Claude announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14) with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back control of his mornings and mental space, and that persistent intelligence running in the background is the direction that matters most.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about Dots, the new personal agent product, and what it means for how we work. Altman described how his dot now handles the overnight chaos that used to pollute his brain first thing in the morning: "I trust my dot to go have good judgment about what I need to think about, not think about. If there is something very urgent, otherwise handle things for me. And I get this space for kind of like creativity or to just hang out with my kids back."

He shared a striking example: during the keynote, a colleague's dot messaged five minutes before the demo warning that a set change would break it and offering to fix it. Another time, his dot found a screenshot he had given up searching for, having patiently looked through image-react overnight. "I would have given up on that," Altman said.

On the bigger picture, Altman framed the choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance, arguing the renaissance was centered on people while the industrial revolution was centered on machines. He is hopeful AI brings a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation while keeping people in control of their lives. He also noted that developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either, and that latent demand in the world surprised him on the upside.

The conversation also covered Space, OpenAI's bet on office suite tools designed for human-AI collaboration, where live documents update automatically and multiple people's dots can work together to create something emergent. Altman described his personal dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, which has shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security today.

On the Decisions API and the race with new labs, Altman said OpenAI wants to make the best, cheapest, fastest model at every point on the Pareto curve, but new architectures will emerge that they cannot cover all of. He remains bullish on the application layer: "There are way more good ideas outside of our building than inside."

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back his mornings and his deep work time, and that is the clearest sign yet of what persistent agents actually change.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about the 22 launches, the difference between an industrial revolution and a renaissance, and what dots change about how he works. Altman said the coding models have not reduced demand for developers: "People just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high." He noted that developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

On the human-centered question, Altman observed that we simply do not care that much about what machines do. "We're obsessed with other people. We want to know about the person behind the art or the person behind the company or the idea or the product." He pointed to the Navier Stokes example, where 10,000 agents worked for four days in perfect coordination, and almost none of the public reaction was about the problem being solved.

The most personal thread was about his dot, Dottie. Altman described how checking his phone first thing polluted his brain and cost him his deep work period, and how he now trusts his dot to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He told a story about a Slack search he gave up on after 10 or 20 minutes, only for his dot to find it overnight, a screenshot he had forgotten was a screenshot. "I would have given up on that." He also described leaving a long voice note while drifting off and waking up to find the thing built, plus the next seven steps done.

On Space, Altman said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple people's AIs work together. He is most excited about live documents that update automatically and that many dots can work on together. "Seven people's dots can working together create something new that kind of existed in all of our heads to some degree, but was like an emergent thing."

On the platform question, Altman said OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent, but has figured out how to build a platform many people build value on. He framed the split between those who believe there will be one company and those who believe in a renaissance of many. On the industrial revolution versus renaissance distinction, he said the former was centered on machines and the latter on people, and that he gets sad hearing people say AI will leave humans as house cats. His hope is a wave of discovery, art, and company formation where people get back in control of their lives.

keywords: AI agents | model race | platform shift
headline: OpenAI's Dev Day Flood Meets Gemini 4
deck: Two model giants trade blows as ChatGPT opens its platform to builders, while practitioners argue over what actually gets automated and what stays stubbornly human.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights
**Core insight**: The day's conversation is split between two platform earthquakes: Google's Gemini 4 launch and OpenAI's Dev Day blitz of 22 releases, including ChatGPT-native apps, MCP server building, and the Dots personal agent. Underneath the model race, builders are converging on a harder truth: the bottleneck is no longer building software but connecting it, deploying it into real workflows, and deciding what humans should still do.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER
**Thibault Sottiaux** (Codex & ChatGPT, OpenAI) announced that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through ChatGPT, with access restrictions or public sharing. He also said **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed.

**Guillermo Rauch** (CEO, Vercel) argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise from provider promos or dubious Zero Data Retention claims, positioning **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, backed by 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He also pushed **Connect**, a way for services to reach 20M+ developers and their agents, fixing the static-credentials liability that plagues agent building.

**Aaron Levie** (CEO, Box) made the case that the real opportunity is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy, since changing enterprise workflows takes far more work than anyone realizes. He listed the full stack of work: moving legacy systems to cloud, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating evals, and continually updating as new models ship, calling it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** (President & CEO, Y Combinator) shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy on how **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence is augmenting air traffic control, predicting that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Duffy's framing: controllers still make every call, and the problem is "dumb AI, not smart AI."

**Nan Yu** (product, Codex, OpenAI) contrasted internal cultures after a week at OpenAI, highlighting an absurdly high bias towards action where a casual feature discussion ends with a PR at your desk and lunch invites land within five minutes. He noted the downside of everything being behind a hair trigger, but said you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets, saying you cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23. He also noted his podcast landing on a top list among industry legends and beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Peter Yang** said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but noted they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (Antigravity) and personal agent (Spark).

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** learnings now power skills in Gemini App, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks with custom instructions in chat. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Peter Steinberger** changed inter-agent communication visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, finding it increasingly irritating in the chat stream. He also called out the fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, saying it is time to rethink how we work.

**Aditya Agarwal** (General Partner, SPC) said the question he keeps asking founders is "What is the maximalist version of this company?", arguing that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and dent the future. He called this the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Nikunj Kothari** (partner, FPV Ventures) said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job.

**Matt Turck** (VC, FirstMark Capital) noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Amjad Masad** (CEO, Replit) highlighted making and one-click publishing Meta VR apps.

**Thariq** (Claude Code, Anthropic) shared prototype work on a game character, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet, and that a real game would need 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters total.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots personal agent gave him back his mornings, and that the real divide is between an AI industrial revolution and an AI renaissance.

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about the 22 launches, the future of engineering, and why people remain at the center of everything. Altman's core observation is that demand is not shrinking: "People just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing." Developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

The most personal thread is Altman's use of **Dots**, the personal agent launched that day. He described losing his early mornings to checking his phone for overnight disasters, polluting his brain before deep work. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest, giving him back creative space and time with his kids. He recounted his dot finding a Slack screenshot he had given up searching for, after Codex failed, because it patiently scanned image-react overnight.

On the platform question, Altman framed two schools of thought among model companies: those who believe there will only be one company, and those like OpenAI who bet on a renaissance of many companies and one-person teams. He admitted OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent, but has figured out how to let millions build significant products on top. The **Decisions API** came from pushing Luna's number one request, cheaper and faster, and from Diogo's long-running ideas, with Jev's approach inspiring more prioritization in that direction.

Altman also touched on **Space**, the office suite bet, saying software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together. He described live documents that update automatically and where seven people's dots can create something emergent that existed only vaguely in everyone's heads. On the industrial revolution versus renaissance framing, he said the former was centered on machines and the latter on people, and he gets sad hearing that AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats. His hope is a wave of discovery, art, and company formation where people stay in control of their lives.

headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Move Into the Chat Stream
deck: Google's new model draws instant praise while OpenAI pushes MCP servers and persistent dots, and builders debate what the applied layer of AI actually looks like.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is dominated by Google's Gemini 4 launch and the rapid expansion of agentic surfaces, from MCP servers built inside ChatGPT to persistent "dots" that run in the background. The emerging trend is less about raw model capability and more about the deployment layer: how agents get connected to real systems, how they coordinate with each other, and how enterprises absorb them without ripping apart legacy workflows.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it to three words and a link: "Gemini 4 Argon!" The VP at Google offered no further detail, letting the launch speak for itself.

**Peter Yang** was direct in his verdict: "Google cooked on Gemini 4!" He added that the remaining gap is on the coding harness with **Antigravity** and the personal agent **Spark**, suggesting the model is ahead of the surrounding product surfaces.

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted to anyone they choose or shared publicly. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice the speed served yesterday. Separately, he described asking his dot to paint him, then to find a recent picture of him online after the first attempt came back cartoonish.

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, described an "absurdly high bias towards action": casual feature discussions end with a PR waiting at your desk, and a Slack lunch suggestion produces a calendar invite within five minutes. He noted the downside of everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but argued the energy is nearly impossible to teach or recover once lost, and that it persists despite the company's size.

**Guillermo Rauch** argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise from provider promos and dubious zero data retention claims, positioning **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows given real usage across 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He also pushed **Connect** as a way for services to reach 20M+ developers and their agents, framing static credentials as both a DX nightmare and a massive liability.

**Aaron Levie** made the case that the real opportunity is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He listed the work involved: moving legacy systems to the cloud, updating data access, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating and maintaining evals, and continually updating as new models arrive. His conclusion: AI is not the same as deploying software, because you are delivering work output inside a process, which opens new firm types and makes it a great time to be an FDE.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them, and airports that can take 15 planes in a 15 minute window while airlines schedule 32. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which Duffy said predicts that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Tan's takeaway: none of this needs a breakthrough, just the will to use software and accept AI augmentation.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software engineering, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted the absurdity of landing on a top podcast list among industry legends and beating MKBHD despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Nikunj Kothari** automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part. He is still looking for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to share demos.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking founders at SPC is "What is the maximalist version of this company?" His argument: AI makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup, so starting small makes funding, hiring, and impact harder.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** is being folded into **Skills** in Gemini App, with Skills rolling out globally as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted that users can now make and one-click publish Meta VR apps on Replit.

**Matt Turck** posted a chart showing SaaS companies watching Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting the graphics and feel are not yet high quality enough to play, that a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and 8-10 characters total, and that he has tried 2-3 variations to fit a big jump into the control scheme.

**Peter Steinberger** changed the visibility of inter-agent communication in the OC harness to a single expandable line, finding the chat stream increasingly irritating, and predicted others will follow. He also noted a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down.

**Dan Shipper** said Every will be ready to vibe check the new model and tagged Sundar Pichai asking to try it.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week on Oct 6-8 and Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Sam Altman says persistent agents gave him back his mornings, and that is the real product story here.** On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about dots, the Decisions API, and why the app layer keeps winning.

Altman described the problem the dots solved for him personally: running a big company means something goes wrong overnight, and checking his phone first thing polluted his brain and killed his deep work window. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He recounted a demo failure during the keynote where Thibault Sottiaux's dot messaged him five minutes before the demo to warn that a set change would break it and asked whether to try to fix it. In another case, his dot found a screenshot in Slack he had spent 10-20 minutes searching for with Codex and given up on, having quietly scanned image-react overnight.

On the platform strategy, Altman said OpenAI cut several planned launches just to fit the 50 minute keynote, and framed the bet as a renaissance rather than an industrial revolution: "I get sad to hear people talk about, oh, AI is going to automate everything. There's going to be no role for humans." He contrasted the two schools among model companies, those who hope there will only be one company and those who believe in a renaissance of small teams, and placed OpenAI firmly in the second camp. On the Decisions API, he credited former OpenAI researcher Diogo for pushing the idea for a long time, and noted that the number one request on Luna was always cheaper and faster, with Jev's emergence making that direction more urgent to prioritize.

Asked what he looks for as an investor, Altman said he was always founder-driven: find the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects and be willing to take a lot of risk with them. He also argued the startup conveyor belt has not re-examined how much work one or two people can now get done. His closing image was of bringing fire down from the mountain and handing it to everybody.

headline: OpenAI Opens the Platform, Builders Take the Wheel
deck: DevDay floods the zone with new surfaces, dots get personal, and a fierce debate brews over whether AI eats SaaS or spawns a renaissance of tiny teams.
quote: I think there will be some areas of work where there's actually not much creativity required and we don't care about the person doing it. And many of those tasks or jobs will be done by AI.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights
**Core insight**: The conversation today is about the platform layer opening up. OpenAI's DevDay deluge of launches, from dots to a Decisions API, signals a shift from model capability to ecosystem enablement, with builders now able to bring their own subscriptions and deploy MCP servers directly through ChatGPT. The counter-current is a growing focus on the unglamorous deployment layer, where the real work of integrating agents into enterprise workflows and connecting them securely is just beginning.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER
**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that users can now build and deploy MCP servers directly through ChatGPT, with configurable access controls. He also noted that GPT-6.1 Sol is the most demanded model ever, causing heavy load that the team is working to alleviate, aiming for nearly double the speed of the previous day. He shared a personal anecdote of his dot painting him, demonstrating its ability to find a recent picture online to improve its output.

**Guillermo Rauch** positioned Vercel AI Gateway as the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing real usage from 400k+ paying customers and a policy of turning down dubious "free token" promos and questionable Zero Data Retention claims. He also promoted Vercel Connect as a solution for securely connecting services to 20M+ developers and their agents, addressing the "DX hell" and liability of static credentials.

**Aaron Levie** argued that the massive opportunity is in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He detailed the extensive work required to change enterprise workflows, from moving legacy systems to the cloud and reengineering processes for agents to managing evals and HITL. He sees this opening up new services firm opportunities and creating a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared insights from a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event. Duffy highlighted the inefficiencies in air traffic management, where controllers use paper charts and six screens, and cited the tool SMART from Air Space Intelligence as a predictive solution that won against bigger companies. Tan noted Duffy's response to critics: "it's dumb AI, not smart AI," emphasizing that controllers still make every call.

**Nan Yu** contrasted internal cultures, noting that after one week at OpenAI, the standout is an "absurdly high bias towards action." He described casual feature discussions resulting in PRs by the time you return to your desk and lunch invites appearing on calendars within minutes, acknowledging downsides but praising the persistent energy at a large company.

**Swyx** praised Flow for doing for hardware engineering what Git+GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders for complex pipelines from cars to rockets. He also celebrated being on a top podcast list among industry legends, beating MKBHD, and questioned the ethics of cropping out logos without attribution in professional tech media.

**Peter Yang** reacted to Google's Gemini 4 launch, saying "Google cooked," but noted they need to be more competitive on coding harness (Antigravity) and personal agent (Spark). He also shared the dichotomy of being called a shill in YouTube comments versus being appreciated by people he met at DevDay.

**Google Labs** announced that Opal's learnings are now supporting the global launch of skills in GeminiApp, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks. As skills roll out, Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, with the team thanking users for shaping the future of customizable AI.

**Nikunj Kothari** shared that he has automated most of his job except for sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he finds the hardest part. He also put out a call for demos of insanely good multiplayer experiences.

**Peter Steinberger** expressed irritation with inter-agent communication in the chat stream, changing the visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line. He also noted a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, suggesting it's time to rethink how we work.

**Aditya Agarwal** posed the question he asks everyone at SPC: "What is the maximalist version of this company?" He argued that in today's AI landscape, the only way to build is with the boldest, most audacious version of the idea, as starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent in the future.

**Matt Turck** shared a post showing SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Amjad Masad** announced the ability to make and one-click publish Meta VR apps.

**Claude** announced that Claude Founder House is coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), offering talks, workshops, and office hours for founders, CEOs, and builders.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman believes the future of work is a renaissance, not an industrial revolution, and his new dot, Dottie, is already giving him back his mornings.

In this rebranded episode of the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sits down with Sam Altman at DevDay to discuss the future of engineering, the launch of dots, and the philosophy behind OpenAI's platform strategy. Altman reflects on how, despite predictions that coding models would reduce the number of developers, there are more developers than ever and more work to do. He attributes this to high expectations and latent demand, noting that people have never been able to do more, but the bar has also never been higher. The conversation touches on the human-centered nature of AI, with Altman observing that we are obsessed with other people, not machines, and that this is a positive surprise. He shares a striking example of 10,000 agents working in perfect coordination to solve a problem, yet the public reaction focused on the people involved, not the achievement itself.

Altman details the launch of dots, a new product that has given him back control of his mornings. He describes how he used to check his phone immediately upon waking, polluting his brain with urgent issues and losing his deep work period. Now, he trusts his dot, Dottie, to handle urgent matters and give him space for creativity or to hang out with his kids. He shares a story where his dot noticed he was trying to find something in Slack, kept thinking about it overnight, and found it for him, a screenshot he had forgotten about. This ability to bring context together from across your entire life and do things that would be hard even for an attentive person is what he finds most impressive. He also discusses the Decisions API, inspired by Jev, and the push to make models cheaper and faster for common use cases. On the platform strategy, Altman emphasizes that OpenAI wants to enable a renaissance of companies, not be the only one. He believes there are way more good ideas outside of OpenAI's building than inside, and that's why they are opening up their ecosystem, letting people build apps in ChatGPT and bring their subscriptions wherever they'd like. He is most excited for the applications that no one at OpenAI could imagine today.

The episode also covers the launch of Space, an office suite designed for human-AI collaboration, and the concept of live documents that automatically update as people interact. Altman shares that he has a personal dashboard that is a changing summary of the business, constantly updated based on what he cares about. He expresses a desire to see a visualization of what his dot thinks he cares about over time, and Dan suggests a live dashboard of his mood. Altman concludes by reflecting on the difference between an industrial revolution and a renaissance, hoping for a future where people get the benefits of economic growth but also get back in control of their lives and spend their time the way they want. He notes that although they haven't figured out the App Store equivalent yet, they have figured out how to help millions of people build and create significant new products and companies. He is excited to be a startup investor again, looking for the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects, and willing to take a lot of risk with them. He believes this is a golden age for startups, with an unbelievable amount of significant new companies building on AI.

headline: OpenAI Floods Dev Day as Gemini 4 Fires Back
deck: Sam Altman bets on Dots and a subscription you carry everywhere, Google answers with Gemini 4, and builders argue the real moat is deployment, not models.
quote: I used to love the early mornings... now I trust my dot to go have good judgment about what I need to think about.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: The day's conversation is split between model launches and the messy work of putting agents into real workflows. OpenAI's Dev Day barrage, Gemini 4, and Codex MCP servers show capability is arriving faster than the deployment layer can absorb it. The emerging trend: the moat is shifting from the model itself to the harness, the gateway, and the services layer that actually changes how work gets done.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

Thibault Sottiaux, Codex & ChatGPT at OpenAI, announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted or shared publicly. He also said **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed. He shared a demo of his dot painting him after finding a recent photo online.

Josh Woodward, VP at Google, simply posted "**Gemini 4 Argon**!" pointing to the launch.

Peter Yang, who writes practical AI tutorials, said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

Guillermo Rauch, CEO of Vercel, argued most token aggregators carry extreme noise from token promos or providers that merely claim **ZDR**. He said **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, backed by 400k+ paying customers, thousands of enterprises, and zero markup, and that they turn down dubious "free token" promos weekly. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for static credentials in agent building, and posted "Faster" alongside a link.

Aaron Levie, CEO of Box, argued there is a huge opportunity in being the **deployment layer** for AI into the economy. He listed the work: moving legacy systems to the cloud, updating data access, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating evals, and continuously updating as new models ship. His point: AI is not the same as deploying software, because you are delivering actual work augmentation, which opens new services firms and makes it a great time to be an **FDE**.

Nan Yu, product at Codex, contrasted what companies take for granted internally. One week into OpenAI, he said the standout is an absurdly high bias towards action, where a PR can appear before you're back at your desk and a lunch invite lands within five minutes. He noted the downside of everything being behind a hair trigger, but said you can't teach that energy and it's hard to get back once lost.

Garry Tan, President & CEO of Y Combinator, shared a conversation with Secretary Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC about air traffic management. Duffy described controllers juggling six screens and a paper flight chart, and pointed to **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which can predict that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Tan's takeaway: none of this needs a breakthrough, just the will to use software and accept AI augmentation.

Swyx, affiliated with smol.ai and Cognition, said **Flow** is doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders on complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted being on a top podcast list among industry legends, and questioned tech media cropping out logos without attribution.

Amjad Masad, CEO of Replit, posted that you can now make and one-click publish **Meta VR apps**.

Google Labs announced that **Opal** is shutting down on Nov 17, 2026, with its learnings living on in **skills** in Gemini App, now rolling out globally as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks.

Matt Turck, VC at FirstMark Capital, posted that SaaS companies are seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Nikunj Kothari, partner at FPV Ventures, said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part.

Peter Steinberger, the ClawFather behind OpenClaw, said inter-agent communication in the chat stream is increasingly irritating, so he changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line. He also said it's a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down.

Thariq, of Claude Code at Anthropic, shared prototype work on a game character, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play and that a real game would need 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters.

Aditya Agarwal, General Partner at SPC, said the question he keeps asking founders is "What is the maximalist version of this company?" He argued that starting small makes funding, hiring, and impact harder, and called this the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

Dan Shipper, CEO of Every, teased a vibe check and pointed readers to a piece on Every.

Claude posted that **Claude Founder House** is coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the point of Dots is not automation but getting his mornings, and his deep work, back.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about Dots, Space, the Decisions API, and why he thinks the AI moment looks more like a renaissance than an industrial revolution.

Altman described how his dot, which he calls Dottie, changed his relationship with early mornings. He used to do his hardest creative work then, but running a large company meant overnight problems demanded his phone first thing, polluting his brain before deep work could start. Now he trusts the dot to judge what is urgent and handle the rest, giving him back that space for creativity or time with his kids. He called it a small-sounding but significant improvement to his life.

He gave concrete examples. During the keynote, a colleague's dot messaged five minutes before a live demo warning that a set change would probably break it and offering to fix it. Just before the interview, Altman's own dot flagged one thing he had to pay attention to in his only break, something he never would have found otherwise. In another case, he spent 10 to 20 minutes searching Slack with Codex for something he was sure he had seen, gave up thinking he dreamed it, and the next day his dot had found it overnight, a screenshot rather than text.

On Space, Altman said they did not set out to build three office suite tools, but realized software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together. He still writes new ideas the old-fashioned way to think them through, then lets dots handle follow-ups. He is most excited about live documents that update automatically and that many people's dots can work on together, describing how seven people's dots can create something emergent that existed only vaguely in all their heads. His own daily dashboard is now a changing summary of the business built just for him, and he is curious what his dot thinks he cares about over time, noting his focus shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security.

On the Decisions API, Altman said the number one request for Luna was always cheaper and faster, and the response to Jev pushed them to prioritize that direction. He said Jev's approach is pretty different and OpenAI may not go build something from scratch like it, but they want the best, cheapest, fastest model at every point on the Pareto curve.

Asked about Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription to third-party apps, Altman said he hates signing up for 100 subscriptions and wants full juice AI wherever he goes. He said the biggest reason for the big Dev Day is a deep belief that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside, and he is most excited for the apps nobody at OpenAI could imagine with conviction today. On startups, he said he would love to be an investor again, called it a golden age, and noted that while some startup lore is invariant, how much one or two people can get done has changed and Silicon Valley has not re-examined that enough.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back his mornings and his deep work time, and that is the clearest sign yet of what persistent agents actually change.

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 things in one keynote. Altman reflected that people just want so much more stuff, and developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either. He was never as doomery about jobs as others, but the upside of latent demand surprised him, as did how much people remain at the center of everything happening with AI. His framing: the Renaissance was centered around people, the Industrial Revolution around machines, and he wants this wave to be the former. He gets sad hearing people say AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats.

The most concrete part was his own use of dots. He used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters that got worse if ignored, and checking his phone polluted his brain. Now he trusts his dot to judge what needs his attention and handle the rest. Two examples stood out: during the keynote, a colleague's dot messaged five minutes before a demo warning it would probably fail and offering to fix it; and just before the interview, Altman's dot flagged one thing he had to pay attention to in his only break, something he never would have found. He also described leaving a long voice note while drifting off and waking to find not only the thing built but the next seven steps done. On a Slack search he gave up on, his dot kept looking overnight, checked every screenshot, and found it. Altman noted the attention hacking of social media and phones had gotten way worse for him personally than he realized. On platforms, he said OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent, but has figured out how to help millions build significant products, and that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside.

## 🧭 Daily Insights
**Core insight**: Today's conversation centers on platform power and deployment reality. Google's Gemini 4 launch drew immediate reactions, while OpenAI pushed builders further into its ecosystem with MCP servers, native apps, and portable subscriptions. The emerging trend is a shift from model capability alone to the messy, human-heavy work of wiring agents into real enterprise workflows.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER
**Josh Woodward** kept it short with a single post announcing **Gemini 4 Argon**.

**Thibault Sottiaux** rolled out several updates: builders can now create and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access controls to restrict or share them. He also noted **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being added to nearly double serving speed. He shared a lighter moment of his dot painting him after finding a recent photo online.

**Peter Yang** praised Google for **Gemini 4**, saying they now need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

**Nan Yu** reflected on his first week at **OpenAI**, highlighting an absurdly high bias towards action: casual feature discussions turn into pull requests by the time you return to your desk, and lunch plans get calendar invites within five minutes. He noted the downsides of hair-trigger change but called the energy nearly impossible to teach or recover once lost.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting it is still prototype quality and that he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people. He mentioned wanting a big jump for the character but struggling to fit it into the control scheme across 2-3 variations.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** learnings now power **Skills** in **GeminiApp**, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks with custom instructions in chat. As Skills roll out globally, Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted that you can now make and one-click publish **Meta VR** apps.

**Guillermo Rauch** argued **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing real usage from 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He noted they turn down dubious "free token" promos and prioritize the best providers over the most. He also promoted **Connect** for reaching 20M+ developers, framing it as easier and more secure than static credentials for agents and apps.

**Aaron Levie** laid out the opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI, arguing the work to change enterprise workflows is far greater than realized. He detailed needs from cloud migration and data access to agent-connected software, reengineered workflows, HITL, and evals, concluding it is a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary Duffy on modernizing air traffic management, where **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence predicts and optimizes routing. Duffy's line: "it's dumb AI, not smart AI," with controllers still making every call. Garry also praised **Capy** for enabling agent swarms to speak as peers and self-coordinate.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line. He also noted the fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down.

**Dan Shipper** teased a vibe check and shared a link to a piece on Every.

**Aditya Agarwal** asked founders at SPC about the maximalist version of their company, arguing that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent. He called it the paradox of AI: building is easier but the floor for a great startup is dramatically higher.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14) with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back control of his mornings and mental space, and he sees AI as a renaissance rather than an industrial revolution.

In this episode of the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper talks with Sam Altman at Dev Day about the future of engineering, platform strategy, and how AI is reshaping work. Altman reflects that people just want so much more stuff and expectations are incredibly high, which he calls a wonderful thing. He notes developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

On the human-centered nature of AI, Altman observes that we do not care that much about what machines do and are obsessed with other people, wanting to know the person behind the art, company, or product. He contrasts an industrial revolution centered on machines with a renaissance centered on people, hoping AI leads to the latter where people get back control of their lives.

Altman shares that the Dots, launched today, are the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings. He used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters could worsen if ignored. Now he trusts his dot to judge what needs his attention and handle the rest, restoring space for creativity or hanging out with his kids. He recounts his dot finding a Slack screenshot he had given up searching for, and another instance where his dot flagged a meeting conflict read from Slack that was not on his calendar.

On platform strategy, Altman says OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent but has figured out how to help millions build significant new products. He notes two schools of thought among AI model companies: those hoping there will be only one company, and those like OpenAI betting on a renaissance of many companies. He mentions the Decisions API and the influence of Jev, saying OpenAI wants to make the best, cheapest, fastest model at every point on the Pareto curve.

Altman expresses excitement for applications not yet imagined, believing there are more good ideas outside OpenAI than inside. He says he would love to be a startup investor again, calling it a golden age, and that his MO was always finding the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects and taking risk with them. He pushes back on the narrative that model labs will eat everything, saying the tweet works because people like doomerism, but it never actually happens.

**Aaron Levie** laid out the case for a deployment layer for AI in the economy: legacy systems moving to cloud, data access updates, software connected to agents, workflows reengineered, **HITL** figured out, and evals maintained as new models release. **Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and can cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. **Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, observed an absurdly high bias towards action: casual feature discussions end with a PR at your desk, and lunch plans get calendar invites within five minutes. He acknowledged the downsides of everything being behind a hair trigger, but noted you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. "You cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23 once you've been in Flow."

**Nikunj Kothari** has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part. He is also looking for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to share demos.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line. He also called the fight between CI and GitHub a fierce contest over what slows him down most.

**Aditya Agarwal** keeps asking founders at SPC: "What is the maximalist version of this company?" He argued that starting small makes funding, hiring, and impact harder, calling it the paradox of AI: building gets easier but the floor for being a great startup rises dramatically.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting it is prototype quality and would need 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters for a real game. He said he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people and is still fitting a big jump into the control scheme after 2-3 variations.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** learnings now power skills in Gemini App, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks with custom instructions in chat. As skills roll out globally, Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted making and one-click publishing Meta VR apps on Replit.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots product gave him back his mornings, and that the real constraint on AI's future is not capability but human creative spirit.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 new things, up from nine last year. Altman's core frame is a renaissance versus an industrial revolution: the industrial revolution was centered around machines and turned many people into cogs, while he hopes AI produces a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation with people back in control of their lives. "I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing," he said, adding that developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

The most personal thread was the Dots launch. Altman described how checking his phone each morning polluted his brain and destroyed his deep work window, and how he now trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what needs his attention. In one example, Thibault Sottiaux got a message from his dot five minutes before the keynote warning that a set issue would break the demo and offering to fix it. In another, Altman's dot found a Slack screenshot he had failed to locate with Codex, having patiently scanned image-react overnight. "I would have given up on that," he said.

On platform strategy, Altman acknowledged OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent, but has figured out how to let millions build significant products on top of it. He contrasted two schools of thought among model companies: those who believe there will only be one company, and those who expect a renaissance of one-person and small-team companies. The Decisions API and cheaper, faster models came partly from watching Jev and from Diogo's long-running ideas, though he said OpenAI will focus on the mainline workhorse across the Pareto curve rather than copying new architectures. On Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription to third-party apps, he said simply: "it's your subscription. Like, you should get to use wherever you want." His biggest excitement is for the apps nobody at OpenAI can imagine yet, because there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside.

Garry also praised **Capy** for letting agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, and it is time to rethink how we work.

**Dan Shipper** said they will be ready to vibe check and asked Sundar Pichai to vibe check the model.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking everyone at SPC is what is the maximalist version of this company, arguing that if you start small you will struggle to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent. He called it the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Claude** announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week on Oct 6-8 and Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back control of his mornings and mental space, and that the real shift is from automation fear to a human-centered renaissance.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper interviewed Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 new things. Altman said people just want so much more stuff and expectations are so high, which is wonderful, but developers have never been able to do more while facing a higher bar. He was never as doomery about jobs as others but was surprised by how much latent demand existed and how much people remain at the center of everything happening with AI. He noted some areas of work require little creativity and those tasks will be done by AI, but there will be no shortage of new things to do.

Altman drew a distinction between an industrial revolution centered on machines and a renaissance centered on people, saying he gets sad when people talk about AI automating everything and humans becoming house cats. He is hopeful for a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get the benefits of growth and stay in control of their lives. On the new Dots, he said it is the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings, since he used to love early mornings for deep work but running a big company meant checking his phone and polluting his brain. Now he trusts his dot to have good judgment about what he needs to think about.

He shared a story where his dot found a screenshot in Slack he had given up searching for, having kept thinking about it overnight. He said the ability to bring context together from across your entire life and do things even an attentive person would struggle with has been super impressive, and that Astra was key to getting the model over the intelligence bar. On Space, he said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together, and he is excited about live documents that update automatically and where seven people's dots can create something emergent. He said his dashboard is now a changing summary of the business just for him, and that six months ago he cared more about revenue and growth but now thinks about safety, alignment, and security.

On the Decisions API, he credited Diogo for inspiring the direction and said the number one request for Luna was to be even cheaper and faster, with Jev making them more excited to prioritize that. He said OpenAI wants the best, cheapest, fastest model at every point on the Pareto curve but cannot cover every new architecture. On Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription to app developers, he said it changes the equation for builders who worry about bankrupting themselves on AI costs. He is most excited for the apps nobody has thought of, saying there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside, and that he would love to be a startup investor again, calling it a golden age. He said his whole MO as an investor was to find the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects and take a lot of risk with them, and that the tweet that OpenAI just killed a startup works because people like doomerism, but it never actually happens.

feed: 2026-10-01T17:41:14.502Z

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | Codex
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Leave the Chat Window
deck: Google ships Gemini 4 while OpenAI pushes MCP servers and dots into daily life, and builders debate what the deployment layer of AI actually looks like.
quote: I think we have a lot of conviction that empowering builders and not just trying to be like the one giant evil megacorp is important.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is split between raw model power and the messy work of putting agents to use. Google's Gemini 4 launch drew immediate reactions, but the more interesting thread is builders describing how agents are moving out of chat and into workflows, dashboards, and enterprise deployment. The trend forming is clear: the model is no longer the product, the harness and the integration layer are.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Swyx** celebrated Flow as the tool doing for hardware engineering what Git and GitHub did for software, arguing it aligns thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets, and that you cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23 once you have used it. He also noted his podcast landing on a top list among industry legends, beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Josh Woodward** kept it short with a single post: Gemini 4 Argon.

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that you can now build and deploy MCP servers directly through ChatGPT, with access restricted to anyone you want or shared with the world. He also said GPT-6.1 Sol is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being added to nearly double yesterday's serving speed, and shared a demo of his dot painting him after finding a recent picture online.

**Peter Yang** said Google cooked on Gemini 4, but noted they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (Antigravity) and personal agent (Spark).

**Nan Yu** contrasted internal cultures after a week at OpenAI, highlighting an absurdly high bias towards action where a PR can appear before you return to your desk and lunch invites land within five minutes. He noted the downsides of everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but said you cannot teach that energy and it is hard to get back once lost.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting it is prototype quality and that a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and 8-10 characters total. He said he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people to improve the animations and design, and that he has tried 2-3 variations to fit a big jump into the control scheme.

**Google Labs** announced that Opal shines on in Skills, with skills now supporting the global launch in GeminiApp as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, with the team thanking users for shaping customizable AI.

**Amjad Masad** posted that you can make and one-click publish Meta VR apps.

**Guillermo Rauch** argued that most token aggregators have extreme noise from token promos or providers that claim to be ZDR, positioning Vercel AI Gateway as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows with real usage, 400k+ paying customers, thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He said they turn down free token promos with dubious ZDR claims weekly, and that most providers suck, you want the best ones. He also invited services to join Connect to reach 20M+ developers and billions of agents, arguing static credentials are DX hell and a massive liability.

**Aaron Levie** said there is a huge opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy, since changing enterprise workflows takes far more work than anyone realizes. He listed legacy systems moving to cloud, data access updates, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating evals, and continual updates as new models release. He argued AI is not the same as deploying software because you are delivering work augmentation, not just tools, and predicted new kinds of firms by industry, company size, and problem, calling it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC, where Duffy described air traffic command centers with six screens and a paper flight chart, and airlines scheduling 32 planes for a 15-plane window. The tool is SMART from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes, with Duffy answering criticism by saying it is dumb AI, not smart AI, and controllers still make every call. Garry also highlighted Capy for letting agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate.

**Matt Turck** posted that SaaS companies are seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders primarily in person, and writing pass notes and calls by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job. He also asked for demos of insanely good multiplayer experiences.

**Peter Steinberger** said inter-agent communication in the chat stream is increasingly irritating, so he changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, and it is time to rethink how we work.

**Dan Shipper** said they will be ready to vibe check, and joked he thought the model was already out, tagging Sundar Pichai to vibe check it.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking everyone at SPC is what the maximalist version of this company is, arguing that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent. He called it the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Claude** announced Claude Founder House coming to SF Tech Week on Oct 6-8 and Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots launch is the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings, and he frames AI as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 new things, up from nine last year and four or five the year before. Altman said they cut several launches just to fit the 50 minute keynote, and that they try to practice what they preach: if these tools make people more productive, they better build more themselves.

The conversation turned on whether AI leads to an industrial revolution or a renaissance. Altman said the industrial revolution was centered around machines while the renaissance was centered around people, and that he gets sad hearing people say AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats. He is hopeful for a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get the benefits of growth and stay in control of their lives.

The most personal thread was about Dots. Altman said he used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters could get worse if ignored, and checking his phone polluted his brain. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest, giving him back space for creativity or hanging out with his kids. He described a moment before the podcast where his dot flagged one thing he had to pay attention to between back-to-back meetings, something he never would have found otherwise.

He also told a story about searching Slack with Codex for something he was sure he had seen, failing for 10 or 20 minutes, and giving up. The next day his dot said it had kept thinking about it overnight and found it: a screenshot, not text, which the dot located by looking at image-react across screenshots. Altman said he would have given up on that.

On Space, Altman said they did not set out to build three office suite tools, but realized software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together. He is most excited about live documents that update automatically and that many dots can work with together, describing how seven people's dots can create something emergent that existed in all their heads. He uses a personal dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, and noted that six months ago he cared more about revenue and growth, while now safety alignment and security are on his mind.

On the Decisions API, Altman credited Diogo, who used to work at OpenAI, and said the number one request for Luna was always cheaper and faster, with the response to Jev pushing them to prioritize that direction. He said they want the best, cheapest, fastest, most capable model at every point on the Pareto curve, but new architectures will emerge they cannot cover all of.

On Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription to app developers, Altman said he hates signing up for 100 subscriptions and wants to use full juice AI wherever he goes. He said the biggest reason for the big Dev Day is a deep belief that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside, and he is confident there will be ChatGPT native apps he loves that no one at OpenAI could imagine with conviction today. He added that he would love to be a startup investor again, calling it a golden age, and that his MO was always finding the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects and taking risk with them.

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots are the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings, and that the real constraint on the AI era is not capability but creative spirit.

quote: I used to love the early mornings. It's my favorite time of the day to work. It's like maximum creativity. That's when I would do the really deep, complicated project of the day.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation centers on OpenAI's Dev Day, where 22 launches, a portable ChatGPT subscription, and a Decisions API signal a platform play rather than a product drop. The emerging trend is the "deployment layer" for AI: the unglamorous work of wiring agents into legacy systems, workflows, and enterprise processes. Meanwhile, builders are split on whether AI makes starting a company easier or simply raises the floor on what counts as ambitious.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access controls to restrict or share them.
