keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | Codex
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Take Over the Stack
deck: Google ships Gemini 4 while OpenAI opens MCP servers inside ChatGPT, and builders debate what AI does to the shape of startups and the work itself.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is dominated by Google's Gemini 4 launch and OpenAI's steady platform expansion, from MCP servers built inside ChatGPT to subscription portability for app developers. The emerging thread is that model capability is becoming table stakes while the real competition shifts to harnesses, personal agents, and the deployment layer that diffuses AI into enterprises.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

Josh Woodward, VP at Google, kept it short with a single post: **Gemini 4 Argon**.

Peter Yang, who writes practical AI tutorials and interviews, called out that Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and the personal agent (**Spark**).

Thibault Sottiaux, working on **Codex** and **ChatGPT** at OpenAI, announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** right through ChatGPT, restricting access or sharing them with the world. He also noted that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed. Separately, he described asking his dot to paint him, and after a cartoonish first attempt, prompting it to try harder and find a recent picture of him online.

Nan Yu, working on product and Codex at OpenAI, reflected on his first week there and the absurdly high bias towards action: casual feature discussions end with a PR waiting at your desk, and lunch plans produce calendar invites within five minutes. He noted the downside of everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but argued you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

Guillermo Rauch, CEO of Vercel, argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise from provider promos and dubious Zero Data Retention claims, positioning **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows with real usage, 400k+ paying customers, and zero markup. He also pushed **Connect** as the way to reach 20M+ developers and their agents, fixing the static credential problem that plagues agent building.

Aaron Levie, CEO of Box, laid out the opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy: legacy systems moving to the cloud, data access updates, software connected to agents, workflows reengineered, HITL figured out, and evals maintained. He stressed that AI is not the same as deploying software, since you are delivering actual work augmentation, and predicted a wave of new firms and plays, calling it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

Garry Tan, President and CEO of Y Combinator, shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC, covering how air traffic is managed with six screens and a paper flight chart, and how the tool **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence predicts ground stops. He also highlighted **Capy**, noting he has never seen agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate rather than being pre-arranged top down.

Nikunj Kothari, partner at FPV Ventures, said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job. He also put out a call for anyone building an insanely good multiplayer experience.

Peter Steinberger, the ClawFather behind OpenClaw, found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also joked about the fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down.

Aditya Agarwal, General Partner at SPC and co-founder of Bevel Health, keeps asking founders what the maximalist version of their company is, arguing that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and dent the future. He called this the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

Matt Turck, VC at FirstMark Capital, posted on SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Google Labs announced that **Opal** shines on in Skills, with the learnings from Opal now supporting the global launch of skills in Gemini App. Skills automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions right in chat, and Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

Amjad Masad, CEO of Replit, highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR apps**.

Thariq, working on Claude Code at Anthropic, shared progress on a game prototype, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet, that a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and maybe 8-10 characters, and that he would ideally work on it with a small group of 2-3 people.

Claude announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says people just want so much more stuff, and that is exactly why the bar for builders keeps rising.

In this episode of the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sits down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about dots, the new personal agent product, and what riding the capability curve of coding models has taught him about the future of work. Altman opens by noting that if you had told him four years ago coding models would reach this level, he would have wondered whether there would even be many developers at Dev Day, yet there are more than ever and more work to do than ever. His read is that expectations are simply higher: developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

The conversation turns to the **Dots** launch, which Altman calls the first thing that has given him back control of his mornings and mental space. He used to love early mornings for deep, complicated work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters and phone-checking that polluted his brain before he could do anything creative. Now he trusts his dot, named **Dottie**, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest, and he describes leaving a long voice note while drifting off and waking up to find not only the thing built but the next seven steps done.

Altman shares a story about a Slack search he gave up on after 10 or 20 minutes with **Codex**, only for his dot to tell him the next day that it had kept thinking about it overnight and found the thing, which turned out to be a screenshot rather than text. He also describes a live document that constantly updates as a changing summary of the business just for him, based on what he cares about that day, and muses about wanting a live dashboard of his own mood over years.

On **Space**, the office suite bet, Altman explains they did not set out to build three tools at once, but realized software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together. On the **Decisions API**, he credits Diogo, formerly of OpenAI, for pushing this class of ideas for a long time, and says the response to Jev made them more excited to prioritize fast, low cost models for common use cases. He frames the broader philosophy around empowering builders rather than being the one giant megacorp, and says the biggest reason for Dev Day is the deep belief that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside.

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | deployment layer
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Move Into the Enterprise
deck: Google ships Gemini 4 while OpenAI opens MCP servers inside ChatGPT, and builders argue the real battle is deploying AI into messy enterprise workflows.
quote: We are going to have such a tiny fraction of that vision ourselves that the world has a fundamentally new enabler.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation centers on capability shipping fast and the harder problem of putting it to work. Google's Gemini 4 and OpenAI's MCP servers inside ChatGPT show the model layer racing ahead, while builders like Aaron Levie argue the real value is the deployment layer: rewiring workflows, data access, and human-in-the-loop processes inside enterprises. The emerging trend is that building is getting easier, but the bar for what counts as a great startup, and a great deployment, keeps rising.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

Josh Woodward announced **Gemini 4 Argon**, signaling Google's next flagship model release.

Thibault Sottiaux shared that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access controls to restrict or share them. He also noted **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double serving speed. He described asking his dot to paint him, and after a cartoonish first attempt, it found a recent picture of him online and did better.

Peter Yang said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

Nan Yu, one week into OpenAI, contrasted internal cultures and highlighted an absurdly high bias towards action: casual feature discussions end with a PR at your desk, and lunch plans get calendar invites within five minutes. He noted the downsides of a hair-trigger environment but said you cannot teach that energy, and it is impressive it persists at a company with several massive buildings in San Francisco.

Thariq shared progress on a game prototype, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet. He wants the character to have a big jump but has not fit it into the control scheme across 2-3 variations, and would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people to improve animations and design.

Google Labs announced that **Opal** is shutting down on Nov 17, 2026, with its learnings living on in **Skills** in Gemini App, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat.

Amjad Masad highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR apps**.

Guillermo Rauch argued **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing 400k+ paying customers, thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He said they turn down free token promos with dubious Zero Data Retention claims weekly. He also pushed **Connect** as a way to reach 20M+ developers and billions of agents, fixing the static credential problem that plagues agent building.

Aaron Levie argued there is a huge opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He detailed that legacy systems need cloud migration, data access updates, agent connections, workflow reengineering, HITL design, and continuous evals as new models release. He stressed AI is not the same as deploying software: you are delivering work output in a process, not just tools, opening new services firms and FDE opportunities.

Garry Tan shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic control still using six screens and paper flight charts, and cited **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and can cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. Duffy's answer to critics: it is dumb AI, not smart AI, and controllers still make every call.

Matt Turck noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Nikunj Kothari said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part.

Peter Steinberger found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down.

Dan Shipper said they will be ready to vibe check, and expressed interest in vibe checking the new model with Sundar Pichai.

Aditya Agarwal asked everyone at SPC: what is the maximalist version of this company? He argued that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent, calling it the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

Claude announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots product gave him back control of his mornings, and that the real lesson of AI is that people remain at the center of everything.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper spoke with Sam Altman at Dev Day about riding the increasing capabilities of coding models. Altman said people just want so much more stuff, and while developers have never been able to do more, there has never been a higher bar either. He was never as doomery about jobs as others, but has been surprised by how much latent demand exists and how much people remain at the center of everything with AI. He noted that some tasks with little creativity required will be done by AI, but there will be no shortage of new things to do.

Altman framed the choice as industrial revolution versus renaissance: the industrial revolution was centered around machines, while the renaissance was centered around people. He gets sad hearing that AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats, and is hopeful this is a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get back control of their lives. He said OpenAI launched 22 things at Dev Day, up from nine last year, and cut some launches to fit the 50-minute keynote, practicing what they preach about building more.

On the Dots product, Altman said it is the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings. He used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters and phone checking that polluted his brain. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle things. He shared that during the keynote, Thibault Sottiaux got a message from his dot five minutes before the demo warning that a set issue would break the demo and offering to fix it. Altman also described his dot finding a Slack screenshot he had given up searching for, after it looked at every screenshot overnight.

On Space, Altman said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together. He still writes new ideas the old-fashioned way to think, then has his dot do follow-ups. He is most excited about live documents that update automatically and where many people's dots work together to create something emergent. He has a personal dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, and noted his focus shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security now.

On the Decisions API, Altman said the number one request for Luna was for it to be even cheaper and faster, and the response to Jev made them more excited to prioritize that direction. He said they want the best, cheapest, fastest, most capable model at every point on the Pareto curve, but new architectures will emerge they cannot cover all of. On Codex native apps and bringing ChatGPT subscriptions to app developers, he said it changes the equation for builders worried about bankrupting themselves on AI costs. He is most excited for the apps no one at OpenAI can imagine today, and said he would love to be a startup investor again, calling it a golden age.

headline: OpenAI Opens the Platform, Google Fires Back With Gemini 4

quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is about the platform turn: OpenAI opened ChatGPT to native apps, MCP servers, and portable subscriptions, while Google shipped Gemini 4 and folded Opal's learnings into Gemini Skills. Underneath the launches, builders are converging on a second theme, that the real work is now deployment and coordination, whether that means agents talking to each other as peers, connecting services securely, or handing over the morning inbox to a personal dot.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted to anyone you choose or shared with the world. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both the API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed. Separately, he showed his dot painting him after it found a recent picture of him online, clicking through the dot's own computer.

**Josh Woodward** kept it short: **Gemini 4 Argon**.

**Peter Yang** said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and the personal agent (**Spark**).

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, contrasted what companies take for granted internally and highlighted an absurdly high bias towards action: a casual discussion about a possible feature ends with the PR waiting at your desk, and a Slack "let's grab lunch next week" produces a calendar invite within five minutes. He noted the downside is everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but that this energy is unteachable and hard to recover once lost, and it is impressive it persists at a company with several massive buildings in San Francisco.

**Guillermo Rauch** argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise, from provider promos that train on your data to providers that merely claim **ZDR**, and positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows thanks to real usage, 400k+ paying customers and thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He said they turn down dubious "free token" promos weekly and do not chase having the most providers because most providers suck. He also pushed **Connect** as the way to reach 20M+ developers and their agents, framing connection as the final boss of building now that code is free, and fixing the static-credential liability that plagues agent DX.

**Aaron Levie** laid out the opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI in the economy: legacy systems moving to the cloud, data access updated, software connected to agents, workflows reengineered, HITL figured out, evals generated and maintained, all continuously updated as new models arrive. He stressed that AI is not the same as deploying software, since you are delivering actual work augmentation rather than a tool the company is merely enabled by, which opens new firms and plays by industry, company size, and problem, and makes it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC, where controllers sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them while four planes reach the same runway and only one can land. Duffy's example: see it two hours out, speed up two, slow one down, and each lands when the runway is open, "like, not complicated stuff, but we don't do any of this." The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes; Duffy's answer to critics is that it is "dumb AI, not smart AI," and controllers still make every call. Tan also praised **Capy** for letting agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate, rather than top-down coordinating child agents.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job.

**Peter Steinberger** changed the visibility of inter-agent communication in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line because it had become increasingly irritating in the chat stream, and predicted others will follow. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down, and that it is time to rethink how we work.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** shines on in **Skills**, with skills rolling out globally in Gemini App as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, and the team thanked everyone who built mini-apps or tried Gems for shaping customizable AI.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR apps**.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking everyone at SPC is "What is the maximalist version of this company?", arguing that today the only way to build is with the boldest, most audacious version of the idea, because starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and dent the future. He called this the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play, that a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and maybe 8-10 characters, and that he would ideally work on it with a small group of 2-3 people. He also said he wants the character to have a big jump but has not fit it into the control scheme in a way he likes across 2-3 variations.

**Dan Shipper** said they will be ready to vibe check, and joked that he thought the model was already out, asking Sundar Pichai to let him vibe check it.

**Claude** announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours with the team for founders, CEOs, and builders.

**Swyx** noted that **Flow** is doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software engineering, enabling acceleration by aligning thousands of stakeholders on complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets, and that you cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23 once you have been in Flow. He also expressed disbelief at being on the top podcast list among industry legends and beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots have given him back his mornings, and that the platform bet is about empowering builders rather than becoming the one giant megacorp.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 new things by Shipper's count, up from nine last year and four or five the year before. Altman said they cut several planned launches just to fit the 50 minute keynote, and framed the volume as practicing what they preach: if these tools make people more productive and people want more stuff, they had better build more themselves.

The conversation turned on the industrial revolution versus renaissance framing. Altman argued the renaissance was centered on people and the industrial revolution on machines, and that he gets sad hearing people say AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats. What he is hopeful for is a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get the economic benefits and also get back control of their lives. He was never as doomery about jobs as others, but has been surprised on the upside by how much latent demand exists and how much people remain at the center of everything happening with AI.

The most personal thread was the Dots. Altman described how he used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters that got worse if ignored, and checking his phone polluted his brain with everything else. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what he needs to think about and handle the rest, giving him back space for creativity or hanging out with his kids. He recounted his dot messaging him five minutes before the keynote to warn that a set change would probably break the demo and offering to fix it, and another time flagging the one thing he had to pay attention to in a break he did not know he had.

The standout story was a Slack search failure. Altman spent 10 or 20 minutes with Codex trying to find something he was sure he had seen, gave up thinking maybe he dreamed it, and the next day his dot told him it had kept thinking about it overnight and found it, a screenshot rather than text, after patiently scanning every screenshot. "I would have given up on that," he said. He also described leaving a long voice note while drifting off and waking up to find not only the thing built but the next seven steps done, and a personal live dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, which shifted from revenue and growth six months ago toward safety, alignment, and security.

On platform strategy, Altman said there are two schools among model companies: those who hope there will only be one company in the world, and those like OpenAI who believe in a renaissance of one-person and small-team companies. He admitted they have not figured out the App Store equivalent, but have figured out how to help millions build significant products. He was most excited about the apps nobody at OpenAI can imagine today, and said he would love to be a startup investor again, calling it a golden age and describing his MO as finding the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects and taking a lot of risk with them. On what changes for startups, he said some things are invariant, like make something people want, but how much work one or two people can get done feels totally different, and Silicon Valley's startup conveyor belt has not re-examined that enough.

https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLuMcoKK9mKgHtW_o9h5sGO2vXrffKHwJL

keywords: agents | platform | coding
headline: OpenAI Opens the Platform as Gemini 4 Lands
deck: Dev Day hands builders the same tools OpenAI uses, Google answers with Gemini 4, and everyone argues about where the real moat now sits.
quote: I think there will be some areas of work where there's actually not much creativity required and we don't care about the person doing it. And many of those tasks or jobs will be done by AI.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights
**Core insight**: The day's conversation is about who owns the platform layer. OpenAI used Dev Day to hand builders the same tools it uses internally, from MCP servers built right inside ChatGPT to apps that can spend a user's own subscription, while Google answered with Gemini 4. The emerging trend is that model capability is becoming table stakes and the fight is shifting to harnesses, gateways, and deployment into real enterprise workflows.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER
**Thibault Sottiaux** of OpenAI announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted to anyone you want or shared with the world. He also said **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model the company has ever shipped across both API and subscriptions, and that capacity is being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed. He shared a demo of his dot painting him after being asked to try harder and find a recent picture online.

**Josh Woodward**, VP at Google, kept it short with a single post: **Gemini 4 Argon**.

**Peter Yang** said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and the personal agent (**Spark**). He also noted the split between people he met at DevDay who love his channel and YouTube commenters calling him an AI shill.

**Nan Yu**, working on product and **Codex** at OpenAI, contrasted what different companies take for granted in their internal cultures. After about a week at OpenAI, the standout is an absurdly high bias towards action: a casual discussion about a possible feature ends with the PR waiting at your desk, and a Slack "let's grab lunch next week" produces a calendar invite within five minutes. He noted the downside is everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but that you cannot teach this energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

**Guillermo Rauch**, CEO of Vercel, argued most token aggregators carry extreme noise from provider promos or providers that merely claim to be ZDR. He positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, citing real usage, 400k+ paying customers and thousands of enterprises, and zero markup, and said they turn down "free token" promos with dubious Zero Data Retention claims every week. He also pushed **Connect** as a way for services to reach 20M+ developers and the agents they ship, framing static credentials as both DX hell and a massive liability.

**Aaron Levie**, CEO of Box, laid out the opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He listed what actually has to change: legacy systems moved to the cloud, data organization and access updated, software connected to agents in new ways, workflows reengineered, HITL figured out, evals generated and maintained, and the whole system continually updated as new models arrive. His key distinction is that AI is not the same as deploying software, since you are delivering actual work augmentation rather than a tool the company is merely enabled by, which opens new kinds of firms and makes it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan**, President and CEO of Y Combinator, shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them, and gave the example of four planes reaching the same runway at once where only one can land, something he said could be seen two hours out and resolved by speeding up two and slowing one down. He cited **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which he said competed with the biggest companies and won, and answered criticism about handing air traffic control to AI by saying controllers still make every call. Tan's conclusion: none of this needs a breakthrough, it needs the will to use software and accept AI augmentation instead of a paper chart and copper wire. He also praised **Capy** for letting agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate rather than being top-down coordinated.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software engineering, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets, and said you cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23 once you have been in Flow. He also noted being on a top podcast list among industry legends and even beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Nikunj Kothari**, partner at FPV Ventures, said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders in person because Zoom sucks, and writing pass notes and calls by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job. He is also still looking for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to reply with a demo.

**Peter Steinberger** said inter-agent communication in the chat stream has become increasingly irritating, so he changed visibility in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line, and predicted others will follow. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down, and that it is time to rethink how we work.

**Aditya Agarwal**, General Partner at SPC, said the question he keeps asking everyone is what the maximalist version of this company is. His argument: if you start small today you will struggle to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent in the future, and this is the paradox of AI, which makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Matt Turck**, VC at FirstMark Capital, noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** shines on in Skills, with skills now rolling out globally in Gemini App as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. The experiment will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, and the team framed it as Opal living on in every skill built next.

**Amjad Masad**, CEO of Replit, highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR apps**.

**Dan Shipper**, CEO of Every, said they will be ready to vibe check and tagged Sundar Pichai saying he would love to vibe check the new model.

**Thariq** of Claude Code shared progress on a game prototype, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play, that a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and maybe 8-10 characters total, and that he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people. He also described struggling to fit a big jump into the control scheme across 2-3 variations.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week on Oct 6-8 and Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops, and office hours for founders, CEOs, and builders.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, is the first thing that has given him back control of his mornings, and he frames the whole moment as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance.**

In the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about what riding the capability curve of coding models has taught him about the future of work. Altman's answer is that people just want so much more stuff, expectations are extremely high, and developers have never been able to do more while the bar has never been higher. He said he was never as doomery about jobs as others, but has still been surprised on the upside by how much latent demand existed and how much people have remained at the center of everything happening with AI. Some areas of work require little creativity and nobody cares who does them, and many of those will go to AI, but he sees no shortage of new things to go do. He pointed to the Navier Stokes example where 10,000 agents worked in perfect coordination for four days and almost none of the public reaction was about the problem being solved, only about the interpretation and the people involved, as evidence that we are obsessed with other people rather than what the machines do.

On the platform question, Altman said OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent but has figured out how to build a platform many people create value on top of. He split AI model companies into two schools: those who believe or hope there will only be one company in the world, and those like OpenAI who believe in a renaissance of many companies and one-person or small-team companies. He said they cut several launches because everything had to fit in 50 minutes, and that if they claim these tools make people more productive, they better build more themselves. On the industrial revolution versus renaissance framing, he said the renaissance was centered on people and the industrial revolution on machines, and that he gets sad hearing people say AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats. His hope is a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get the economic benefits and also get back control of their lives.

The most concrete thread was **Dots**. Altman said his dot, Dottie, is the first thing that has given him back his mornings and mental space, because he trusts it to judge what he needs to think about and handle the rest, instead of polluting his brain with everything overnight. He described a message from Thibault Sottiaux's dot five minutes before a live demo warning that a set was probably going to break the demo and offering to fix it, and his own dot flagging one thing he had to pay attention to in a rare break, something he never would have found otherwise. He said **Astra** was the key research piece, since they needed a smart enough model before the rest of the system could work. He also described dumping raw voice notes at night and waking up to find not only the thing built but the next seven steps done, and a case where his dot kept thinking overnight and found a screenshot he had given up searching for in Slack. On **Space**, he said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together, and that he still writes new ideas the old-fashioned way to think, then has his dot do the follow-ups. His favorite artifact is a live dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, which shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security today. On the **Decisions API**, he credited Diogo, a former OpenAI employee, for pushing the class of ideas for a long time, and said the response to JEV made them more excited to prioritize super fast, super low cost models for common use cases, while acknowledging new architectures will exist that they cannot cover all of. On Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription to app developers, he said he hates signing up for 100 subscriptions and that your subscription should work wherever you want. His biggest excitement is for the apps nobody at OpenAI can imagine today, because there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside, and he said he would love to be a startup investor again, calling it a golden age.

quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights
**Core insight**: Builders are converging on the same conclusion: models are commoditizing fast, and the scarce value is now in the deployment layer, the harness, and the connective tissue that gets AI into real workflows. OpenAI's Dev Day pushed agents, apps, and platform access to the center, while founders argue that implementation, not intelligence, is the new bottleneck.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER
Thibault Sottiaux, Codex and ChatGPT at OpenAI, announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted or shared publicly. He also said **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed.

Guillermo Rauch, CEO of Vercel, argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise from promos or providers merely claiming **ZDR**, and positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, backed by 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for static credentials plaguing agent builders, aiming to reach 20M+ developers.

Aaron Levie, CEO of Box, laid out the case that the big opportunity is being the **deployment layer** for AI into the economy. He noted that moving legacy systems to the cloud, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, and maintaining evals is far more work than anyone expects, and predicted a wave of new firms and FDE plays.

Nan Yu, product at Codex at OpenAI, contrasted internal cultures and highlighted OpenAI's absurdly high bias towards action, where a casual feature discussion can end with a PR waiting at your desk. He admitted the hair-trigger pace has downsides but argued the energy is nearly impossible to teach or recover once lost.

Garry Tan, President and CEO of Y Combinator, described a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy about **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts air traffic and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. Duffy's framing: controllers still make every call, and the problem is not a breakthrough but the will to use software.

Swyx praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git and GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted his podcast ranking among industry legends despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

Peter Yang said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness **Antigravity** and personal agent **Spark**.

Google Labs announced that **Opal** learnings now power the global launch of **skills** in Gemini App, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks with custom instructions in chat. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

Peter Steinberger, building OpenClaw and working with OpenAI, changed inter-agent communication visibility in his harness to a single expandable line, betting others will follow. He also said the fight between CI and GitHub is what slows him down most, and it is time to rethink how we work.

Nikunj Kothari, partner at FPV Ventures, said he has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part.

Aditya Agarwal, General Partner at SPC, argued the only way to build a company today is the boldest, most maximalist version of the idea, because starting small makes funding, hiring, and impact all harder. He called it the paradox of AI: building is easier, but the floor for greatness has risen dramatically.

Matt Turck, VC at FirstMark Capital, flagged SaaS companies watching Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Amjad Masad, CEO of Replit, highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR** apps.

Thariq, working on Claude Code at Anthropic, shared prototype work on a game character, noting the graphics and feel are not yet playable quality and that he would want 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters total for a real game.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot gave him back his mornings, and that the real unlock is not automation but people staying at the center of everything AI does.

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about persistent intelligence, the platform bet, and why humans keep mattering. Altman described launching 22 things in one keynote, up from nine last year, and said they cut items just to fit the 50 minutes, because if OpenAI tells people these tools make them more productive, it had better build more itself.

The most personal thread was about **Dots**, the new agent product. Altman said it is the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings, a period he used to protect for deep creative work before running a large company meant overnight disasters. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He recalled a demo breaking during the keynote and a colleague's dot messaging five minutes before, aware of the presentation, warning the set would break the demo and offering to fix it.

He also described giving up on a Slack search after 10 or 20 minutes with Codex, only for his dot to find it overnight, patiently scanning image-react screenshots because the thing he remembered was an image, not text. On **Space**, he said software was never designed for humans and multiple AIs to collaborate, and he is most excited about live documents that update automatically and where several people's dots can create something emergent. His own dashboard is now a changing summary of the business, tuned to what he cares about that week, which shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security.

Asked about the race with new labs, Altman said OpenAI wants the best, cheapest, fastest model at every point on the Pareto curve, but acknowledged new architectures will exist that it cannot cover all of. On the app layer, he said he would love to invest again, that he has never seen startups get successful this quickly, and that the biggest reason for the big Dev Day is a deep belief that there are more good ideas outside the building than inside.

Quote: "I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing."

keywords: agents | platform | coding models
headline: OpenAI Opens the Platform as Dots Take Over
deck: Dev Day rains 22 launches, ChatGPT learns to build MCP servers, and Sam Altman hands mornings back to his dot while Gemini 4 lands.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI
## 🧭 Daily Insights
**Core insight**: The conversation has shifted from raw model capability to the platform and deployment layer. OpenAI's Dev Day flood of launches, from building MCP servers inside ChatGPT to letting users bring their subscriptions into third-party apps, signals that the labs now compete on how much they empower builders rather than on benchmarks alone. Meanwhile, practitioners are converging on a harder truth: agents are easy to demo and hard to deploy, and the real work is wiring them into legacy systems, workflows, and human-in-the-loop processes.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER
**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted to anyone you choose or shared with the world. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed.

**Josh Woodward** kept it short with a single line: "Gemini 4 Argon!" Peter Yang followed up saying Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, though they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

**Guillermo Rauch** argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise, from provider promos to dubious **Zero Data Retention** claims, and positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows with 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for the static-credential mess plaguing agent builders, noting code is now free and connecting is the final boss.

**Aaron Levie** made the case that the huge opportunity is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He listed what actually has to change: legacy systems moved to cloud, data access updated, software connected to agents, workflows reengineered, **HITL** figured out, evals generated and maintained. His conclusion: AI is not the same as deploying software, and it is a great time to be an **FDE**.

**Nan Yu**, about a week into OpenAI, contrasted internal cultures and highlighted an absurdly high bias towards action, where a casual feature discussion ends with a PR on your desk and a lunch suggestion becomes a calendar invite within five minutes. He noted the downsides of everything being behind a hair trigger, but said you cannot teach that energy.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC, covering how **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence predicts air traffic and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. Duffy's framing: controllers still make every call, and the alternative is paper charts and copper wire.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders on complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted his podcast charting among industry legends without optimizing for Apple Podcasts.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** is shutting down on Nov 17, 2026, with its learnings living on in **Skills** inside Gemini App, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat.

**Peter Steinberger** changed inter-agent communication visibility in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line, finding the chat stream increasingly irritating, and joked about the fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down.

**Nikunj Kothari** automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job.

**Aditya Agarwal** asked everyone at SPC what the maximalist version of their company is, arguing that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and dent the future. His paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR** apps on Replit. **Matt Turck** observed SaaS companies watching Anthropic's ARR growth flatline. **Thariq** shared prototype notes on a game character, wanting a big jump that fits the control scheme, and noted he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people to make it real.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back his mornings, and that is the clearest sign yet of where persistent agents are heading.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about the 22 launches, the platform bet, and what **dots** actually change. Altman framed the moment as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance, and he is betting on the latter. "I get sad to hear people talk about, oh, AI is going to automate everything. There's going to be no role for humans," he said. "That's certainly not what we think."

The most concrete thread was personal. Altman described how checking his phone each morning used to pollute his brain and destroy his deep work window. Now he trusts his dot to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He recounted his dot finding a screenshot in Slack that he had given up on, after Codex failed to locate it, because the dot kept looking overnight. "I would have given up on that," he said.

On the platform side, Altman explained the bet behind letting people build apps in ChatGPT, bring their subscriptions anywhere, and access a marketplace. He admitted OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent, but argued they have figured out how to let millions build significant products. He also touched on **Space**, the office suite bet, and the idea of live documents that update as people and their dots interact, where seven people's dots can create something emergent that existed only in everyone's heads.

On models, he confirmed the **Decisions API** was inspired in part by Diogo's long-running ideas and the response to JEV, and said OpenAI will keep pushing cheap and fast for common use cases. His investor instinct remains founder-driven: find the absolute best founders on ambitious projects and take a lot of risk with them. "I've never seen startups get as successful as quickly," he said.

https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLuMcoKK9mKgHtW_o9h5sGO2vXrffKHwJL

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | AI platform
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Take Over the Stack
deck: Google fires its biggest model shot yet, OpenAI opens MCP servers and apps inside ChatGPT, and builders debate what the agent era does to startups and enterprise work.
quote: I think we have figured out how to help millions of people build and create significant new products and companies.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: The day's conversation is split between raw model power and the scaffolding around it. Google's Gemini 4 launch drew immediate praise, but builders quickly pivoted to the harder questions: coding harnesses, personal agents, and how agents actually get wired into real work. The emerging consensus is that models are becoming table stakes while the deployment layer, from MCP servers to enterprise workflow reengineering, is where the next wave of value gets built.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it short and loud with a single post announcing **Gemini 4 Argon**, signaling Google's latest flagship model has arrived.

**Peter Yang** called out that Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued the company still needs to get more competitive on its coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**) to fully capitalize.

**Thibault Sottiaux** rolled out several updates: users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT** with configurable access, and **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed. He also shared a lighter moment of his dot painting him after being asked to try harder.

**Guillermo Rauch** made the case that **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing 400k+ paying customers, zero markup, and a policy of turning down dubious "free token" promos and questionable Zero Data Retention claims. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for static credentials plaguing agent development, framing connection as the final boss of building now that code is effectively free.

**Aaron Levie** argued there's a huge opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy, noting that changing enterprise workflows takes far more work than anyone realizes. He laid out the full scope: legacy systems moving to cloud, data access updates, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating evals, and continuous updates as new models release. His conclusion: this opens new kinds of firms and plays, and it's a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC, where Duffy described how air traffic management still runs on paper charts and copper wire. Duffy pointed to **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes, while insisting controllers still make every call and dismissing critics with "it's dumb AI, not smart AI."

**Nan Yu** reflected on his first week at OpenAI, highlighting an absurdly high bias towards action where PRs appear before you're back at your desk and lunch invites land within five minutes. He acknowledged downsides of everything being behind a hair trigger, but noted you can't teach that energy and it's unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

**Nikunj Kothari** shared what he's automated away and what remains: sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part of the job.

**Aditya Agarwal** posed the question he keeps asking founders at SPC: what is the maximalist version of this company? He argued that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent, calling it the paradox of AI that building is easier but the floor for being a great startup has dramatically risen.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted his podcast ranking among industry legends without optimizing for Apple Podcasts.

**Matt Turck** posted a pointed observation about SaaS companies watching Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Peter Steinberger** changed inter-agent communication visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, finding the chat stream increasingly irritating, and noted a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting it's still prototype quality and that making a real game would require 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters total.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** learnings now power skills in Gemini App, with skills rolling out globally as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Amjad Masad** announced one-click publishing of Meta VR apps from Replit.

**Dan Shipper** teased being ready to vibe check and asked Sundar Pichai for the opportunity.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14) with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back his mornings and his mental space, and it's the clearest sign yet that persistent, proactive agents are becoming the default interface to work.

In this episode of the Every Podcast (formerly AI & I), Dan Shipper sits down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about the 22 things OpenAI launched, the future of engineering, and why he thinks we're heading toward a renaissance rather than an industrial revolution. Altman opens by reflecting on how coding models have exceeded even his expectations: "People just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing." He notes that developers have never been able to do more, but there's never been a higher bar either.

The conversation turns to the Dots, OpenAI's new personal agent product, and Altman gets personal about what it's changed for him. He describes how checking his phone first thing used to pollute his brain and destroy his deep work period, but now he trusts his dot to exercise judgment about what needs his attention. "I trust my dot to go have good judgment about what I need to think about, not think about. If there's something very urgent, otherwise handle things for me. And I get this space for kind of like creativity or to just hang out with my kids back." He shares a striking example: his dot noticed he'd been searching Slack for something and failing, then spent the night looking through every screenshot and found it, something he would have given up on.

On the platform strategy, Altman draws a distinction between two schools of thought among AI model companies: those who believe there will only be one company in the world, and those like OpenAI who believe we'll see a renaissance in the number of companies. "We want to go as hard as we can with the platform to enable people to do that." He acknowledges they haven't figured out the App Store equivalent yet, but believes they've figured out how to help millions of people build significant new products.

The discussion covers the Decisions API and how Jev, a new type of model, changed how OpenAI thinks about building internally. Altman credits Diogo, a former OpenAI employee, for inspiring this direction over a long time, and notes that the number one request for Luna was always to be cheaper and faster. He's candid that OpenAI can't cover every new architecture: "There will be new approaches, new architectures, new technologies that are things we just can't cover all of. But for the mainline workhorse, we will try to deliver that."

On startups, Altman is visibly energized. "I would love to be a startup investor again. It is just a golden age." He describes his investing MO as finding the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects and being willing to take a lot of risk with them. He pushes back on the narrative that model companies will eat everything: "The tweet works because people like Doomerism, but it never actually happens." His explanation is partly conviction about empowering builders and partly his own background: "We really do love developers. I think that's partly I just also love startups, and that's where I came from."

The episode closes with Altman reflecting on the renaissance versus industrial revolution framing. He sees the renaissance as fundamentally centered around people, while the industrial revolution was centered around machines, and he's hopeful AI leads to the former: "People get to, back in control of their lives and ability to kind of do the things they want and spend their time the way they want."

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the point of AI is not replacing people but giving them back control of their time and attention, and his own dot, Dottie, is the first tool that has done that for his mornings.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about coding models, platforms, and the future of work. Altman said that despite fears of automation, there are more developers than ever and more work to do than ever, because "people just want so much more stuff" and expectations keep rising. He framed the choice as industrial revolution versus renaissance, arguing the renaissance was centered on people while the industrial revolution was centered on machines, and that he wants AI to expand human potential rather than turn people into cogs.

Altman described the new **Dots** product as the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings. He used to check his phone immediately and pollute his brain with everything that happened overnight, losing his deep work period. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He shared a story where his dot noticed he had been searching for something in Slack, kept thinking about it overnight, and found it, a screenshot he had forgotten was an image, not text. He also described jotting raw notes and voice memos to his dot, which built five or six versions of a feature he could iterate on, and waking up to find the thing built plus the next seven steps done.

On platforms, Altman said OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent but has figured out how to let millions build significant products. He contrasted two schools of thought: those who believe only one company will exist, and those who believe in a renaissance of many companies and one-person firms. He said the biggest reason for Dev Day is a deep belief that there are way more good ideas outside OpenAI's building than inside, and that he is most excited for apps he cannot imagine today. On the **Decisions API** and fast, cheap models, he said the number one request for Luna was to be even cheaper and faster, and that OpenAI will keep pushing the Pareto curve of cost, speed, and modality, while acknowledging new architectures like Jev's approach are things they cannot cover all of.

He also touched on **Space**, the office suite bet, saying software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together. He described live documents that update automatically and where seven people's dots can create something emergent that existed in all their heads. Asked what he cares about now, he said safety, alignment, and security, versus revenue and growth six months ago, and he wants a way to visualize how his dot thinks his priorities evolve over time.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets, and said you cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23 once you have used it.

He described how checking his phone each morning used to pollute his brain and destroy his deep work window, and how he now trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. **Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders on complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets. He also celebrated landing on a top podcast list among industry legends, beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part. He is still hunting for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to send demos.

**Aditya Agarwal** keeps asking founders at SPC one question: "What is the maximalist version of this company?" His argument is that starting small now means struggling to fundraise, hire great people, and dent the future. He called it the paradox of AI: building is easier, but the floor for being a great startup rose dramatically.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down, and that it is time to rethink how we work.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** lives on in **Skills**, now launching globally in Gemini App as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. Opal itself shuts down on Nov 17, 2026, a bittersweet farewell to the experiment that taught them how people customize workflows.

**Amjad Masad** noted you can now make and one-click publish **Meta VR** apps on Replit.

**Matt Turck** shared a chart suggesting SaaS companies are watching Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Thariq** gave a candid look at his game prototype, saying the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet. He estimated a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and 8-10 characters total, and that he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people.

**Dan Shipper** teased that Every will be ready to vibe check the new model and said he would love Sundar Pichai to let them try it.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week on Oct 6-8 and Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops, and office hours for founders, CEOs, and builders.

## 🎙 PODCASTS
**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says persistent agents like Dots gave him back his mornings, and that the real question for builders is no longer whether you can build something but what is worth building.

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at DevDay, where OpenAI launched 22 new things, up from nine last year and four or five the year before. Altman admitted they cut several planned launches just to fit the 50 minute keynote, and framed the volume as practicing what they preach: if these tools make people more productive and people want more stuff, OpenAI better build more itself.

The centerpiece of the conversation was **Dots**, the persistent agent Altman now uses daily. His dot is named Dottie, and he described how it restored his early morning deep work by filtering overnight chaos: "I trust my dot to go have good judgment about what I need to think about, not think about." He recounted a demo-breaking production bug that a colleague's dot flagged five minutes before the keynote, and a Slack screenshot he had failed to find with Codex that his dot located overnight after patiently scanning image-react data. Altman said Dots required a smart enough model, pointing to **Astra** as the key research unlock.

On **Space**, the three-tool office suite bet, Altman said software was never designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together. His favorite concept is live documents that update automatically and let several people's dots co-create something emergent. He now keeps a personal dashboard that constantly rewrites itself based on what he cares about, which recently shifted from revenue and growth toward safety, alignment, and security.

The platform thread ran through **MCP servers**, native apps, and letting users bring their ChatGPT subscription into third-party apps. Altman said the biggest reason for the big DevDay is a deep belief that there are more good ideas outside the building than inside, and that he wants to be a startup investor again because it is a golden age. He rejected the "OpenAI kills startups" narrative, arguing the doom tweet works because people like doomerism but it never actually happens. His closing frame was Renaissance versus Industrial Revolution: one centered on people, the other on machines, and he wants AI to deliver the growth without turning people into cogs.

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | Codex
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Move Into the Chat Stream
deck: Google's new model draws instant praise while OpenAI ships MCP servers inside ChatGPT and Sam Altman says his dot gave his mornings back. The fight now shifts to harnesses, platforms, and who owns the deployment layer.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is less about raw model capability and more about the scaffolding around it. Google's Gemini 4 launch drew immediate praise, but builders quickly pivoted to the missing pieces: coding harnesses and personal agents. Meanwhile, OpenAI pushed platform moves (MCP servers in ChatGPT, subscription portability) and Sam Altman described how a personal agent reclaimed his mornings, a concrete signal that persistent, proactive agents are moving from demo to daily habit.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it to two words and a link: "Gemini 4 Argon!" The VP at Google, overseeing Google Labs, GeminiApp and GoogleAIStudio, let the launch speak for itself, and the reaction across the timeline suggests it landed.

**Peter Yang** called it plainly: "Google cooked on Gemini 4!" The practical AI tutorial writer argued the remaining gaps are not the model but the surrounding product, naming the coding harness **Antigravity** and the personal agent **Spark** as the places Google still needs to get more competitive.

**Thibault Sottiaux** of Codex and ChatGPT at OpenAI shipped two notable platform updates. Users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through ChatGPT, with access restricted to anyone they choose or shared with the world. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model "pretty much ever" across both the API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed.

**Nan Yu**, one week into his role on product for Codex at OpenAI, contrasted internal cultures and landed on one standout: an "absurdly high bias towards action." A casual feature discussion ends with a PR on your desk; a Slack "let's grab lunch" produces a calendar invite within five minutes. He acknowledged the downside of everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but argued you cannot teach that energy, and once lost it is unbelievably hard to get back.

**Guillermo Rauch** made the case for **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, citing real usage, 400k+ paying customers and thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He said the team turns down weekly "free token" promos from companies with dubious **Zero Data Retention** claims, and noted they would rather have the best providers than the most. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for agents plagued by static credentials, calling the final boss of building the act of connecting to stuff.

**Aaron Levie** argued the biggest opportunity right now is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He walked through the real work: moving legacy systems to the cloud, updating data access, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out **HITL**, and maintaining evals as new models ship. His core distinction: with AI agents you are delivering actual work augmentation, not deploying a well understood tool, which opens new kinds of firms and makes it a great time to be an **FDE**.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart, and airlines scheduling 32 planes into a 15-plane window. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which he said predicts that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Duffy's answer to critics: "it's dumb AI, not smart AI," with controllers still making every call.

**Nikunj Kothari** of FPV Ventures listed what survived his personal automation push: sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job. He added he has no EA and never intends to hire one.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also called the fight between **CI** and **GitHub** over what slows him down most a reason to rethink how we work.

**Aditya Agarwal** of SPC and Bevel Health posed the question he keeps asking founders: "What is the maximalist version of this company?" His read on the paradox of AI is that it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup, so starting small now makes funding, hiring and impact harder.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** is shutting down on Nov 17, 2026, with its learnings living on as **skills** in GeminiApp, now rolling out globally as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat.

**Amjad Masad** of Replit highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR** apps.

**Matt Turck** of FirstMark Capital posted a chart on SaaS companies watching Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Thariq** of Claude Code shared progress on a game prototype, noting it is prototype quality and that a real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and 8-10 characters total, plus help from a small group of 2-3 people on animation and design.

**Dan Shipper** of Every teased being ready to vibe check the new model and asked Sundar Pichai for access.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets. He also celebrated landing on a top podcast list among industry legends without optimizing for Apple Podcasts.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway: Sam Altman says his personal agent, a dot named Dottie, gave him back his mornings, and that is the clearest sign yet that persistent agents are becoming daily infrastructure rather than demos.**

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 things in a 50 minute keynote, up from nine last year and four or five the year before. Altman's framing was that capability gains have not reduced demand for developers but raised the bar: "People just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing." He said he was never as doomery about jobs as others, but has been surprised on the upside by how much latent demand existed and how much people remain at the center of everything happening with AI. His Renaissance versus Industrial Revolution analogy ran through the conversation: the Renaissance was centered on people, the Industrial Revolution on machines, and he wants AI to land on the former, with people back in control of their time.

The most concrete thread was the dot. Altman described losing his early morning deep work window to overnight crises and phone checking, and how trusting his dot to judge what is urgent restored that space. He recounted a live demo breaking during the keynote and a colleague's dot messaging five minutes before to warn the set would break the demo and offer to fix it. He described leaving a long voice note while drifting off and waking to find the thing built plus the next seven steps done, and a dot that spent the night hunting through image-react for a screenshot he had given up finding in Slack. On platforms, he said OpenAI has not figured out the App Store equivalent but has figured out how to let millions build on top, and that the bet is on a renaissance of many companies rather than one model company eating everything. He also touched on the **Decisions API**, the number one request that **Luna** be even cheaper and faster, and his belief that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside.
