keywords: Gemini 4 | Codex | agents
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Codex Hits Its Limits
deck: Google ships Gemini 4 while OpenAI races to add capacity, and builders debate what the agent era actually changes about work, platforms, and the startup floor.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: The day's conversation is split between raw model releases and the messy reality of shipping on top of them. Google's Gemini 4 drew immediate praise even as builders flagged gaps in coding harnesses and personal agents, while OpenAI's Codex team admitted heavy load on GPT-6.1 Sol and raced to double serving speed. Underneath the launches, the recurring theme is that AI makes building easier but raises the bar on what a great startup or product actually looks like.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward**, VP at Google, kept it short with a single post announcing **Gemini 4 Argon**.

**Thibault Sottiaux**, working on Codex and ChatGPT at OpenAI, shared three updates. Builders can now create and deploy **MCP servers** directly through ChatGPT, with access restricted to anyone or shared publicly. He also noted that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both API and subscriptions, and that ChatGPT and Codex were under heavy load before more capacity came online, targeting almost twice the speed of the prior day. Separately, he described asking his dot to paint him, then to try harder and find a recent picture online, which produced a better result.

**Peter Yang**, who writes practical AI tutorials and interviews, said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued they still need to be more competitive on coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

**Nan Yu**, working on product and Codex at OpenAI and previously head of product at Linear, contrasted what different companies take for granted internally. A week in, he said the standout is an absurdly high bias towards action: a casual feature discussion can end with a PR waiting at your desk, and a Slack lunch invite becomes a calendar meeting within five minutes. He acknowledged downsides, noting everything feels behind a hair trigger and things change out from under you, but said you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost. He found it impressive that the energy persists at a company with several massive buildings in San Francisco.

**Thariq**, working on Claude Code at Anthropic, shared progress on a game prototype. He said the animations and design are fine but someone with expertise could make it much better, and that he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people. He described the work as prototype quality, noting he would go through 3-4 variations of the character and have maybe 8-10 characters in a real game. He also said he wants the character to have a big jump but has not fit it into the control scheme in a way he likes, trying 2-3 variations.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** shines on in Skills. When Opal launched in Google Labs, it was an experiment to learn how people customize workflows, and that creativity inspired Gems made by Labs and now supports the global launch of skills in **GeminiApp**. Skills are a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions right in chat, built from the learnings from Opal. As skills roll out globally, Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Amjad Masad**, CEO of Replit, posted about making and one-click publishing **Meta VR apps**.

**Guillermo Rauch**, CEO of Vercel, argued that most token aggregators have extreme noise from token promos or providers that claim to be ZDR. He positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing real usage from real customers, an at-scale platform with 400k+ paying customers and thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He said they turn down free token promos with dubious ZDR claims every week and do not chase having the most providers, because most providers suck and you want the best ones. In a separate post, he invited services to add themselves to **Connect** to reach 20M+ developers and the billions of agents they will ship, arguing that with code now free, the final boss of building is connecting to stuff, and that Connect fixes the static credential problem that plagues agent building.

**Aaron Levie**, CEO of Box, laid out the opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He argued the work to change out enterprise workflows is far greater than anyone realizes, spanning moving legacy systems to the cloud, updating data organization and access, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating and maintaining evals, and continually updating the system as new models release. He stressed that AI is not the same as deploying software: with agents you are delivering actual work augmentation, not just tools the company is enabled by, which means a completely different implementation and enablement process. He predicted new kinds of firms and plays by industry, company size, and problem, and called it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan**, President and CEO of Y Combinator, shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them, and gave the example of four planes reaching the same runway at once where only one can land, which could be resolved two hours out by speeding up two and slowing one down. He noted an airport that can take 15 planes in a 15 minute window while airlines schedule 32, and said he is handing that data to airlines to spread out. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which he said competed with the biggest companies and won, and which predicted a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Duffy's answer to criticism about handing air traffic control to AI: it is dumb AI, not smart AI, and controllers still make every call. Tan also praised **Capy** for letting agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate, rather than top-down coordinating child agents.

**Matt Turck**, VC at FirstMark Capital, posted about SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Nikunj Kothari**, partner at FPV Ventures, said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders primarily in person, and writing pass notes and calls by hand, which he called frankly the hardest part of the job. He added that he does not have an EA or intend to hire one.

**Peter Steinberger**, building OpenClaw and working with OpenAI, said inter-agent communication in the chat stream is increasingly irritating, so he changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, and predicted others will follow. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, and that it is time to rethink how we work.

**Dan Shipper**, CEO of Every, posted about being ready to vibe check and shared a piece on Every, and said he thought a model was already out before tagging Sundar Pichai to vibe check it.

**Aditya Agarwal**, General Partner at SPC and co-founder of Bevel Health, said the question he keeps asking everyone at SPC is what the maximalist version of this company is. He argued that if you are building a company today, the only way is to have the boldest, most audacious, maximalist version of the idea and how it can change the arc of the future, because starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent. He called this the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Claude** announced that Claude Founder House is coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours with the team, plus time to meet others building with Claude.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots product is the first thing that has given him back control of his mornings, and he frames the whole moment as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about what riding the capability curve of coding models has taught him about the future of work. Altman's answer was blunt: people just want so much more stuff, expectations are so high, and that is a wonderful thing. Developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either. He said he was never as doomery about jobs as others, but has been surprised on the upside by how much latent demand exists and how much people remain at the center of everything happening with AI. He acknowledged some areas of work require little creativity and will be done by AI, but argued there is no shortage of new things to go do.

The conversation turned to the industrial revolution versus the renaissance framing from his keynote. Altman said the renaissance was fundamentally centered around people and the industrial revolution around machines, and that many people did not have a purely positive personal impact from the latter, becoming cogs in a machine disconnected from what made life good. He said he gets sad hearing people say AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats, and that what he is hopeful for is a huge new wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get the benefits of growth and also get back control of their lives.

The most concrete part was about Dots, the new product launched that day. Altman said it is the first thing that has given him back control of his mornings and mental space. He used to love early mornings for deep, complicated work, but running a big company means something is always going wrong overnight, and checking his phone polluted his brain with everything else. Now he trusts his dot to judge what he needs to think about and handle the rest, which he called a significant improvement to his life despite sounding small. He described a demo failure during the keynote where Thibault Sottiaux's dot messaged him five minutes before the demo warning that a set change would probably break it and asking if it should try to fix it. He also described his dot reminding him of one thing he had to pay attention to before an interview, something he never would have found otherwise.

On Space, the three office suite tools launched that day, Altman said they did not set out to build them, but realized software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together. He said he still writes new ideas the old-fashioned way because it helps him think, then has his dot do the follow-ups. His favorite new concept is live documents that update automatically as people interact or the world changes, where seven people's dots can work together to create something emergent that existed in all of their heads. He described a personal dashboard that is a changing summary of the business based on what he cares about, updating constantly from his Slack messages. He said six months ago he cared more about revenue and growth, and now a lot of his mind is on the safety, alignment, and security challenge, and he would love to see what his dot thinks he cares about most over time.

On the Decisions API, Altman credited Diogo, who used to work at OpenAI, with talking about this class of ideas for a long time. He said the number one request for Luna was for it to be even cheaper and faster, and the response to Jev made them more excited to prioritize that direction. He said OpenAI wants to make the best, cheapest, fastest, most capable model at every point on the Pareto curve of cost, speed, and modality, but that new approaches and architectures will emerge that they cannot cover all of. On Codex native apps and bringing ChatGPT subscription tokens to app developers, he said he hates signing up for 100 subscriptions and that your subscription should work wherever you want. He said he is most excited for the applications nobody at OpenAI could imagine with conviction today, and that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside. He called it a golden age for startups and said he would love to be a startup investor again, looking for the absolute best founders working on ambitious projects while being willing to take a lot of risk with them.

https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLuMcoKK9mKgHtW_o9h5sGO2vXrffKHwJL

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back control of his mornings, and that persistent agents connected to your systems are the direction that matters most.

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about dots, the future of work, and why AI feels more like a renaissance than an industrial revolution. Altman said people just want so much more stuff and their expectations are so high, which he called a wonderful thing, even as the bar for developers has never been higher. He was never as doomery about jobs as others, but has been surprised on the upside by how much latent demand exists and how much people remain at the center of everything happening with AI.

The most personal thread was about Dottie, his dot. Altman used to love early mornings for deep, complicated work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters could get worse if ignored. Checking his phone polluted his brain and killed the deep work period. Now he trusts his dot to have good judgment about what he needs to think about, and if something is very urgent, otherwise handle things for him. He described a dot messaging Thibault five minutes before the keynote demo to warn that a production issue would break it and ask if it should try to fix it. Another time, his dot told him he had one little break to pay attention to one thing he never would have found otherwise.

Altman also described a feature idea he could not have explained in enough detail to get built. He jotted notes to his dot, which built five or six versions, and he iterated with continuous feedback. He told a story about searching Slack with Codex for something he was sure he had seen, failing, and giving up. The next day his dot said it had kept thinking about it overnight and found it: a screenshot, not text, which it located by looking at every screenshot. Altman said he would have given up on that.

On Space, Altman said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and their AIs work together. He is most excited about live documents that update automatically and that many dots can work on together, creating something emergent that existed in all of our heads. He has a personal dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, and noted he cared more about revenue and growth six months ago, now more about safety, alignment, and security. On the Decisions API, he credited Diogo and said the response to JEV made them more excited to push toward super fast, super low cost models for common use cases. He closed by saying there are way more good ideas outside their building than inside, and that he would love to be a startup investor again because it is a golden age.
https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLuMcoKK9mKgHtW_o9h5sGO2vXrffKHwJL

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | platform
headline: OpenAI Opens the Platform as Gemini 4 Lands
deck: Two giants move at once: Google ships Gemini 4 while OpenAI hands builders MCP servers, native apps and portable subscriptions. Meanwhile the dot agents quietly take over the workday.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is about the platform turn. OpenAI is opening ChatGPT to MCP servers, native apps and portable subscriptions, while Google answers with Gemini 4 and folds the Opal experiment into Gemini Skills. The underlying theme is that agents are moving from demos to infrastructure, and the bottleneck is no longer model capability but deployment, connection and trust.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted to anyone you choose or shared with the world. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both the API and subscriptions, and that capacity is being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed. He shared a lighter moment too: asking his dot to paint him, then pushing it to try harder and find a recent picture of him online.
https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2105519215092584786
https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2105464274747527543
https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2105395200248156556

**Josh Woodward** kept it short with a single post: **Gemini 4 Argon**.
https://x.com/joshwoodward/status/2105396307250782446

**Peter Yang** said Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but argued they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and the personal agent (**Spark**). He also noted the split reaction to his DevDay presence, with attendees loving the channel while YouTube comments call him an AI shill.
https://x.com/petergyang/status/2105393240392585359
https://x.com/petergyang/status/2105486915147645063

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, described an absurdly high bias towards action: a casual discussion about a possible feature ends with the PR waiting at your desk, and a Slack lunch suggestion becomes a calendar invite within five minutes. He acknowledged the downsides of everything being behind a hair trigger, but argued you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.
https://x.com/thenanyu/status/2105316751802348005

**Guillermo Rauch** positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing real usage from 400k+ paying customers, thousands of enterprises and zero markup. He said Vercel turns down free token promos with dubious Zero Data Retention claims every week, and that most providers suck, you want the best ones. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for static credentials in agent building, opening access to 20M+ developers.
https://x.com/rauchg/status/2105476587756138942
https://x.com/rauchg/status/2105390544096841942

**Aaron Levie** argued there is a huge opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. Legacy systems need to move to the cloud, data access needs updating, software needs connecting to agents, workflows need reengineering, HITL needs figuring out and evals need maintaining. His key point: with AI agents you are delivering actual work augmentation, not just deploying software, which opens new kinds of firms and makes it a great time to be an FDE.
https://x.com/levie/status/2105354449795621179

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart, and airports that can take 15 planes in a 15 minute window while airlines schedule 32. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, predicting that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes, with controllers still making every call.
https://x.com/garrytan/status/2105395957357588561

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** shines on in **Skills**, with skills rolling out globally in **GeminiApp** as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, and the team thanked everyone who built mini-apps or tried Gems.
https://x.com/GoogleLabs/status/2105352889665564838

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software engineering, aligning thousands of stakeholders for complex, irreversible, high value pipelines from cars to rockets. He also celebrated landing on a top podcast list among industry legends and beating MKBHD, while noting he does nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.
https://x.com/swyx/status/2105348724331606411
https://x.com/swyx/status/2105419486383964544

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes and calls by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job.
https://x.com/nikunj/status/2105532229866942772

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also called the fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down a reason to rethink how we work.
https://x.com/steipete/status/2105362785534361996
https://x.com/steipete/status/2105341288958869952

**Aditya Agarwal** shared the question he keeps asking at SPC: what is the maximalist version of this company. He argued that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people and make a dent, calling it the paradox of AI, which makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.
https://x.com/adityaag/status/2105339569139056776

**Matt Turck** observed SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.
https://x.com/mattturck/status/2105261211906384132

**Amjad Masad** noted you can make and one-click publish Meta VR apps.
https://x.com/amasad/status/2105321112389443805

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting it is all prototype quality and the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet. He said making a real game would take 3-4 variations of the character and maybe 8-10 characters total, and that he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people.
https://x.com/trq212/status/2105333509246386546
https://x.com/trq212/status/2105333507711271060
https://x.com/trq212/status/2105333506226503710

**Claude** announced that Claude Founder House is coming to SF Tech Week on Oct 6-8 and Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops and office hours for founders, CEOs and builders.
https://x.com/claudeai/status/2105370680485417401

**Dan Shipper** teased that Every will be ready to vibe check the new model, and said he thought it was already out.
https://x.com/danshipper/status/2105443942330503416
https://x.com/danshipper/status/2105391711950393423

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the dot agents gave him back his mornings, and that is the clearest signal yet that persistent, proactive intelligence is the next platform shift.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about dots, the platform turn and why AI looks more like a renaissance than an industrial revolution. Altman opened by rejecting the doom framing around engineering jobs: people just want so much more stuff, expectations are so high, and developers have never been able to do more even as the bar keeps rising. He noted that OpenAI launched 22 things at this Dev Day, up from nine last year and four or five the year before, and admitted they cut several launches just to fit the 50 minute keynote, because if the tools make people more productive, OpenAI better build more itself.

The most personal thread was about his dot, named Dottie. Altman described how checking his phone first thing used to pollute his brain and destroy his deep work window, and how he now trusts the dot to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He told a story about a feature he wanted but could not explain in enough detail to get built, so he jotted notes and voice memos to his dot, which went off and built five or six versions he could iterate on. He also described a Slack search that Codex failed to find, only for his dot to report the next day that it had kept thinking overnight and found it, a screenshot he had forgotten was an image rather than text.

On platforms, Altman said OpenAI has not yet figured out the App Store equivalent, but has figured out how to let millions build on top. He framed the split in the industry between those who hope there will be one company and those who believe in a renaissance of many, and said the goal is to give builders the same tools OpenAI uses. He was most excited about the apps nobody has imagined yet, calling it a deep belief that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside.

The conversation also touched on Space, the office suite bet, where Altman said current abstractions break down when multiple people and multiple people's AIs collaborate. He described live documents that update automatically as the world changes, and a personal dashboard that constantly rewrites itself based on what he cares about, which shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment and security today. He called it the best feeling to leave a long voice note while drifting off and wake up to find the thing built and the next seven steps done.

Asked what he would look for as an investor, Altman said he was always founder-driven, backing the absolute best founders on ambitious projects and taking a lot of risk with them. He called the current moment a golden age, said he has never seen startups get as successful as quickly, and closed on the idea that the world has a fundamentally new enabler, something like fire, brought down from the mountain and handed to everybody.
https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLuMcoKK9mKgHtW_o9h5sGO2vXrffKHwJL

keywords: agents | platform | coding
headline: OpenAI Opens the Platform, Gemini 4 Lands
deck: Dev Day hands builders the keys while Google answers with Gemini 4, and the agent era forces a rethink of how software, work, and startups actually get built.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: The day's conversation is all about the platform layer. OpenAI used Dev Day to hand builders the same tools it uses internally, from MCP servers built right inside ChatGPT to subscription portability, while Google shipped Gemini 4 and folded its Opal experiment into Gemini Skills. The emerging thread is that agents are no longer a feature but the substrate, and the real bottleneck is shifting from what can be built to how it all gets connected, deployed, and made coherent.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

Thibault Sottiaux, who works on Codex and ChatGPT at OpenAI, announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through ChatGPT, with controls to restrict access or share them with the world. He also noted that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both the API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed. He shared a lighter moment too, showing his dot painting him after he asked it to try harder and find a recent picture of him online.

Josh Woodward, VP at Google, kept it to three words: **Gemini 4 Argon**.

Peter Yang, who makes practical AI tutorials, celebrated that Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, but said they still need to be more competitive on the coding harness **Antigravity** and the personal agent **Spark**.

Guillermo Rauch, CEO of Vercel, argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise from provider promos or dubious **Zero Data Retention** claims, positioning **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows thanks to real usage from 400k+ paying customers and zero markup. He also pushed **Connect**, urging services to get in front of 20M+ developers and the agents they ship, framing static credentials as both a DX nightmare and a massive liability.

Aaron Levie, CEO of Box, made the case that the huge opportunity right now is being the **deployment layer** for AI into the economy. He laid out the real work: moving legacy systems to the cloud, reconnecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out **HITL**, and maintaining evals as new models ship. His conclusion is that AI deployment is not the same as deploying software, since you are delivering work output inside a process, which opens up new kinds of firms and makes it a great time to be an **FDE**.

Garry Tan, President and CEO of Y Combinator, shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart, and how the tool **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence predicts that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Tan highlighted the contrast between **Capy**, where agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate, and other editors that only let you top down coordinate child agents.

Nikunj Kothari, partner at FPV Ventures, said he has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part of the job. He is also still hunting for an insanely good **multiplayer** experience and asked builders to send demos.

Peter Steinberger, the ClawFather behind OpenClaw, said inter-agent communication in the chat stream was getting irritating, so he changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line and expects others to follow. He also called it a fierce fight between **CI** and **GitHub** over what slows him down, and said it is time to rethink how we work.

Nan Yu, who works on product for Codex at OpenAI, reflected on his first week there and the standout trait of an absurdly high bias towards action. He described casual feature discussions where the PR is waiting by the time you return to your desk, and Slack hellos that turn into calendar invites within five minutes, noting the downsides of a hair trigger but arguing you cannot teach that energy once it is lost.

Aditya Agarwal, General Partner at SPC, said the question he keeps asking founders is what the maximalist version of their company is. His argument is that starting small makes it hard to raise, hire, and dent the future, and that AI creates a paradox by making building easier while dramatically raising the floor on what counts as a great startup.

Matt Turck, VC at FirstMark Capital, pointed to SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Google Labs announced that **Opal** shines on in **Skills**, with the global launch of skills in Gemini App built from the learnings of Opal. The experiment will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, and the team thanked everyone who built mini-apps or tried Gems.

Amjad Masad, CEO of Replit, noted you can now make and one-click publish **Meta VR** apps.

Claude announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week from Oct 6 to 8 and to Stockholm on Oct 14, with talks, workshops, and office hours for founders, CEOs, and builders.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

Sam Altman sat down with Dan Shipper at Dev Day for the rebranded Every Podcast, and the through line is that the platform era of AI is really about giving builders the same tools the labs use, then getting out of the way. The takeaway: even as coding models get dramatically more capable, demand for what people want to build is rising faster than the supply of builders.

Altman framed the moment as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance, and he is betting on the latter. He described the industrial revolution as centered on machines, where many people became cogs disconnected from what made life good, and the renaissance as centered on people reconnecting to human potential. He is openly skeptical of the doom framing, saying he gets sad when people talk about humans becoming house cats while AI decides how to reward us.

The most concrete part of the conversation was about **Dots**, the new product that Altman says gave him back control of his mornings. He used to love early mornings for deep creative work, but running a large company meant overnight disasters that got worse if ignored, and checking his phone polluted his brain with everything else. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He told one story where his dot noticed he had spent 10 or 20 minutes searching Slack through Codex for something he was sure he had seen, failed to find it, and gave up. The next day the dot told him it had kept thinking about it overnight and found it, a screenshot rather than text, after patiently scanning image-react results.

On the platform side, Altman said OpenAI launched 22 things at Dev Day, up from nine last year, and cut several more just to fit the 50 minute keynote. He acknowledged the App Store equivalent has not been figured out, but argued OpenAI has figured out how to let millions build significant products on top of the platform, and that the belief in a renaissance of many companies is what drives opening up the ecosystem. He also touched on **Space**, the office suite bet, saying software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together, and described live documents that update automatically as people and their dots interact. On the **Decisions API**, he credited former OpenAI colleague Diogo for pushing the class of ideas, and said the response to JEV made OpenAI more eager to prioritize making models super fast and cheap for common use cases. His closing note was simple: there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside, and he is most excited for the apps nobody at OpenAI can imagine today.

https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLuMcoKK9mKgHtW_o9h5sGO2vXrffKHwJL

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | Codex
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Move Into the Chat Stream
deck: Google ships its biggest model yet while OpenAI pushes MCP servers and persistent dots, and builders argue the real bottleneck is no longer code but coordination.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is dominated by two things: Google's **Gemini 4** launch and OpenAI's Dev Day wave of platform features, from **MCP servers** built inside ChatGPT to persistent **dots** that work in the background. The emerging thread is that building is no longer the hard part. What everyone is circling is coordination: agent swarms talking to each other, live documents that update themselves, and the deployment layer that gets AI into real enterprise workflows.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it to three words and a link: **Gemini 4 Argon**. The VP at Google Labs let the model do the talking.

**Thibault Sottiaux** had the busiest day of anyone. He announced that you can now build and deploy **MCP servers** right through ChatGPT, with access restricted to anyone you want or shared with the world. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed. Separately, he had his dot paint a portrait of him, and after asking it to try harder and find a recent picture online, it did better.

**Peter Yang** gave the honest Dev Day read: people he met in person loved his channel, YouTube comments called him an AI shill. His verdict on the launch: Google cooked on **Gemini 4**, and now needs to be more competitive on coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, contrasted what companies take for granted internally. The standout is an absurdly high bias towards action: a casual discussion about a possible feature ends with the PR in front of you by the time you return to your desk, and a Slack lunch suggestion becomes a calendar invite within five minutes. He notes the downside of everything feeling behind a hair trigger, but says you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

**Thariq** is prototyping a game and being candid about its state: all prototype quality, graphics and feel not high enough to play. A real version would need 3-4 variations of the character and maybe 8-10 characters total, and he would rather work with a small group of 2-3 people. He is still trying to fit a big jump into the control scheme, having tried 2-3 variations.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** shines on in **Skills**, now launching globally in Gemini App. Skills are a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat, built from the learnings of Opal. The experiment shuts down on Nov 17, 2026.

**Amjad Masad** announced you can make and one-click publish **Meta VR** apps.

**Guillermo Rauch** made the case for **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, citing real usage, 400k+ paying customers and thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He says they turn down free token promos from companies with dubious Zero Data Retention claims every week, and that most providers suck, you want the best ones. He also pushed **Connect**: with code now free, the final boss of building is connecting to stuff, and static credentials given to every agent are both DX hell and a massive liability.

**Aaron Levie** argued there is a huge opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. Legacy systems need moving to the cloud, data access updated, software connected to agents, workflows reengineered, HITL figured out, and evals generated and maintained. His key distinction: with AI agents you are delivering actual work augmentation, not deploying tools the company is merely enabled by, which opens up new kinds of firms and plays. Great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared his conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at the Startup Industrial Base event in DC. At the command center, people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them. Duffy's example: four planes reach the same runway at once and only one can land, so see it two hours out, speed up two, slow one down. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts that a 2.5 hour ground stop might only need 45 minutes. Controllers still make every call. Tan's summary: none of this needs a breakthrough, it needs the will to use software and be ok with AI augmentation instead of a paper chart and copper wire. He also flagged **Capy**, where agent swarms speak to one another as peers and self coordinate rather than being top-down coordinated.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Nikunj Kothari** has automated most of his job away except sourcing and writing outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he calls the hardest part of the job. He is also still looking for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to reply with a demo.

**Peter Steinberger** finds inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also called it a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, and said it is time to rethink how we work.

**Dan Shipper** is ready to vibe check the new model and asked Sundar Pichai for the chance.

**Aditya Agarwal** keeps asking everyone at SPC what the maximalist version of their company is. If you start small today, you will struggle to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent in the future. This is the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back control of his mornings, and that the real shift is not automation but people staying at the center of everything AI touches.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day, where OpenAI launched 22 new things by Dan's count, up from nine last year and four or five the year before. Altman's framing of the moment is that capability is not the constraint. "I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing." Developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

The most personal thread is the **Dots** launch. Altman describes losing his early mornings, his most creative working hours, to the overnight disasters that pile up at a big company. Now he trusts his dot to judge what needs his attention and handle the rest. Two stories stand out: a dot messaging five minutes before the keynote to warn that a set change would probably break the live demo, and a dot that spent the night looking through image-react at every Slack screenshot to find something Altman had given up searching for, because he had misremembered it as text rather than a screenshot. "I would have given up on that."

On **Space**, Altman says software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple people's AIs work together. His favorite new abstraction has no word yet: live documents that update automatically as people interact, where seven people's dots working together create something emergent that existed only vaguely in all their heads. He now keeps a personal dashboard that constantly rewrites itself based on what he cares about, which shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security.

On the **Decisions API**, he credits former OpenAI researcher Diogo for pushing the class of ideas for a long time, and says the response to JEV made them more excited to prioritize cheap, fast models. He does not think OpenAI can copy JEV's approach from scratch, but wants the best, cheapest, fastest model at every point on the Pareto curve.

On platforms, Altman draws the Renaissance versus Industrial Revolution line: one centered on people, the other on machines. He is not doomery about jobs, but he is clear that some tasks with no creativity required and no care about who does them will go to AI. The bet behind letting people build apps in ChatGPT, bring their subscription anywhere, and use the marketplace is that there are way more good ideas outside OpenAI's building than inside. "I am very confident that there will be things that I really do love that I cannot imagine today."

We want to know about the person behind the art or the person behind the company or the idea or the product."

The most concrete part of the conversation was about **Dots**, OpenAI's new personal agent. Altman described how checking his phone each morning used to pollute his brain and destroy his deep work window, and how he now trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what deserves his attention. In one example, his dot noticed he had failed to find something in Slack, kept thinking about it overnight, scanned every screenshot, and found it, a screenshot he had forgotten was a screenshot. "I would have given up on that."

He also described a feature idea he could never have explained well enough to get built, which his dot turned into five or six versions he could iterate on, and a live dashboard that constantly updates based on what he cares about, which has shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment and security today. On **Space**, the office suite bet, he argued current software abstractions break down when multiple people and multiple AIs collaborate, and that live documents updated automatically by many people's dots could produce emergent ideas. On the **Decisions API**, he credited former OpenAI researcher Diogo and the response to Jev for pushing cheaper, faster models, while saying OpenAI cannot cover every new architecture. He closed by saying he would love to be a startup investor again, that he has never seen startups get successful as quickly, and that the tweet claiming OpenAI killed a startup always works because people like doomerism, but it never actually happens.

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | Codex
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as Agents Take Over the Stack
deck: Google's new model draws instant praise while OpenAI pushes MCP servers and GPT-6.1 Sol capacity, and builders debate what the deployment layer of AI actually looks like.
quote: I trust my dot to go have good judgment about what I need to think about, not think about. If there is something very urgent, otherwise handle things for me.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: Today's conversation is split between raw model power and the messy work of putting AI to use. Google's Gemini 4 launch drew immediate praise, but builders spent just as much energy on the plumbing: MCP servers, agent-to-agent coordination, token gateways, and the unglamorous enterprise deployment layer. The trend forming is that capability is no longer the bottleneck; integration, trust, and workflow redesign are.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it to three words and a link: "Gemini 4 Argon!" The VP at Google, Google Labs, GeminiApp, and GoogleAIStudio let the launch speak for itself.

**Peter Yang** called it: "Google cooked on Gemini 4!" He added that Google still needs to be more competitive on its coding harness (Antigravity) and personal agent (Spark), framing the launch as a strong model play with room left on the tooling side.

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, with access restricted to anyone you want or shared with the world. He also noted that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across both the API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to reach almost twice yesterday's speed. In a lighter moment, he described asking his dot to paint him, then to find a recent picture of him online after the first attempt came back cartoonish.

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, described an "absurdly high bias towards action": casual feature discussions end with a PR on your desk, and a Slack lunch suggestion turns into a calendar invite within five minutes. He acknowledged the downsides of everything being behind a hair trigger, but argued you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

**Guillermo Rauch** argued that most token aggregators carry extreme noise from provider promos and dubious Zero Data Retention claims. He positioned **Vercel AI Gateway** as the largest trustworthy datasource on global AI token flows, citing 400k+ paying customers, thousands of enterprises, and zero markup, and said the company turns down free token promos weekly. He also pushed **Connect** as a way for services to reach 20M+ developers and their agents, fixing the static-credential liability that plagues agent building.

**Aaron Levie** laid out the case for the deployment layer: legacy systems moving to cloud, data access updates, software wired to agents, workflows reengineered, HITL figured out, evals maintained. His key point is that AI is not the same as deploying software, because you are delivering actual work augmentation rather than a tool the company merely runs. He called it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart, and airlines scheduling 32 planes into a 15-plane window. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. Duffy's answer to critics: "it's dumb AI, not smart AI," with controllers still making every call.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** as doing for hardware engineering what Git plus GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted the irony of landing on a top podcast list while actively hating Spotify and doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** learnings now power skills in GeminiApp, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat. Opal itself shuts down November 17, 2026, with the team thanking users for shaping customizable AI.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted one-click publishing of Meta VR apps from Replit.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part of the job.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub over what slows him down, and that it is time to rethink how we work.

**Thariq** shared prototype work on a game character, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play yet, that a real game would need 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters, and that he wants a big jump that does not yet fit the control scheme.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking founders is "What is the maximalist version of this company?" His argument: if you start small today, you will struggle to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent. AI makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Dan Shipper** said he thought a model was already out, and asked Sundar Pichai for a vibe check.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back his mornings and his mental space, and that is the clearest sign yet of what persistent agents are actually for.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about Dots, the deployment of persistent intelligence, and why people stay at the center of everything AI touches. Altman pushed back on the doom framing: "I get sad to hear people talk about, oh, AI is going to automate everything, there's going to be no role for humans. We're going to be like house cats and the AI will decide how to reward us." His counter is a renaissance rather than an industrial revolution, one centered on people rather than machines.

The most concrete thread was Altman's personal use of Dots. He used to love early mornings for deep, complicated work, but running a large company meant overnight disasters that got worse if ignored for an hour. Checking his phone polluted his brain and killed the deep work window. Now he trusts his dot to judge what needs his attention and handle the rest. He described a dot messaging Thibault Sottiaux five minutes before a live demo to warn that a set change would break it, and another time when his dot found a Slack screenshot he had given up searching for, having patiently scanned image-react screenshots overnight.

On Space, Altman said software was not designed for collaborating with AI, especially when multiple people and multiple AIs work together, and that current abstractions break down. He is most excited about live documents that update automatically and that many people's dots can work on together, describing how seven people's dots can create something emergent that existed in all their heads. He also touched on the Decisions API, the number one request for Luna being cheaper and faster, and how Jev's approach is different enough that OpenAI will push the Pareto curve rather than copy it. On the app layer, he said there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside, and that he has never seen startups get as successful as quickly.

The emerging theme is that building is getting easier while the hard part shifts to deployment, coherence, and connecting agents to real systems.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

Swyx celebrated **Flow**, arguing it does for hardware engineering what **Git** plus **GitHub** did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also noted his podcast landing on a top list among industry legends, beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

Josh Woodward of Google announced **Gemini 4 Argon**.

Thibault Sottiaux of OpenAI shared that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through **ChatGPT**, restricting access or sharing publicly. He also noted **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's speed. He described asking his dot to paint him, and after a cartoonish result, prompting it to find a recent photo online for a better attempt.

Peter Yang praised Google for **Gemini 4**, saying they now need to be more competitive on the coding harness (**Antigravity**) and personal agent (**Spark**).

Nan Yu, one week into OpenAI, contrasted internal cultures and highlighted an absurdly high bias towards action, where a casual feature discussion ends with a PR waiting at your desk and lunch invites land within five minutes. He noted the energy persists even as the company spans several massive San Francisco buildings.

Thariq of Claude Code shared progress on a game prototype, noting the graphics and feel are not yet high quality enough to play, and that a real game would need 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters total. He said he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people.

Google Labs announced that **Opal** learnings now power **Skills** in Gemini, a faster way to automate repetitive tasks. As Skills roll out globally, Opal will shut down on Nov 17, 2026.

Amjad Masad of Replit announced one-click publishing of **Meta VR** apps.

Guillermo Rauch of Vercel argued **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing 400k+ paying customers, zero markup, and turning down dubious "free token" promos and questionable Zero Data Retention claims. He also invited services to join **Connect** to reach 20M+ developers and their agents, fixing the static-credential liability plaguing agent building.

Aaron Levie of Box argued the huge opportunity is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy, since changing enterprise workflows takes far more work than realized. He detailed the long list: moving legacy systems to cloud, updating data access, connecting software to agents, reengineering workflows, figuring out HITL, generating evals, and continually updating as models improve. He called it a great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

Garry Tan shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC, who described air traffic command centers with six screens and paper flight charts. Duffy cited **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes, insisting controllers still make every call.

Matt Turck noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

Nikunj Kothari of FPV Ventures said he has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part. He is still looking for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to share demos.

Peter Steinberger found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating, changing visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line. He also said it is a fierce fight between CI and GitHub on what slows him down, and it is time to rethink how we work.

Dan Shipper of Every said they will be ready to vibe check, and joked he thought a model was already out, inviting Sundar Pichai to let him vibe check it.

Aditya Agarwal of SPC asked founders what the maximalist version of their company is, arguing that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and dent the future. He called this the paradox of AI: it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

Claude announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots product gave him back his mornings, and he frames AI as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance.

On the rebranded Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at Dev Day to talk about the future of engineering, the launch of Dots, and why people stay at the center of everything. Altman reflected that people just want so much more stuff, and that developers have never been able to do more while facing a higher bar. He said he was never as doomery about jobs as others, but was surprised on the upside by how much latent demand existed and how much people remain central to AI.

The conversation turned to the industrial revolution versus the renaissance. Altman argued the renaissance was centered on people while the industrial revolution was centered on machines, and that he gets sad hearing people say AI will automate everything and leave humans as house cats. He is hopeful for a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people stay in control of their lives.

Altman described the Dots launch as the first thing that gave him back control of his mornings and mental space. He used to love early mornings for deep work, but running a big company meant overnight disasters and phone-checking that polluted his brain. Now he trusts his dot, named Dottie, to judge what is urgent and handle the rest. He shared a striking example: during the keynote, a colleague's dot messaged five minutes before a demo warning it would likely fail and offering to fix it. Another time, his dot noticed he had been searching Slack for something and, overnight, found it, a screenshot he had forgotten was an image.

On the Decisions API and the Jev model, Altman said the number one request for Luna was for it to be even cheaper and faster, and that seeing the response to Jev pushed them to prioritize that direction. He said OpenAI wants to make the best, cheapest, fastest, most capable model at every point on the Pareto curve, but cannot cover every new architecture. He also discussed bringing your ChatGPT subscription to app developers, saying it is your subscription and you should get to use it wherever you want.

Asked what excites him at the application layer, Altman said he is most excited for the ones they have not thought of, citing a deep belief that there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside. He called it a golden age for startups and said he would love to be a startup investor again, always founder-driven and willing to take risk on ambitious projects. He closed by noting that although they have not figured out the App Store equivalent, they have figured out how to help millions build significant new products and companies.

**Nan Yu**, one week into OpenAI, described an absurdly high bias towards action. A casual discussion about a possible feature ends with the PR sitting in front of you by the time you get back to your desk; a Slack lunch suggestion becomes a calendar invite within five minutes. He acknowledged the downside, everything behind a hair trigger and things changing out from under you, but argued you cannot teach that energy and it is unbelievably hard to get back once lost.

He said the team turns down free token promos from companies with dubious Zero Data Retention claims every week, and that most providers suck, you want the best ones. Separately he pushed **Connect** as the fix for static credentials in agent building, calling the final boss of building the act of connecting to stuff.

**Aaron Levie** argued there is a huge opportunity in being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. Legacy systems need cloud migration, data access needs updating, software needs connecting to agents, workflows need reengineering, HITL needs figuring out, and evals need maintaining. His core distinction: software deployment let the customer keep running, while AI agents deliver actual work augmentation, a completely different enablement process. Great time to be an FDE or FDE firm.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic command centers where people sit in front of six screens with a paper flight chart above them, and airports that can take 15 planes in a 15 minute window while airlines schedule 32. The tool is **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence, which predicts and can cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. Duffy's answer to critics: it's dumb AI, not smart AI, and controllers still make every call.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git and GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders across complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. You cannot go back to spreadsheet_final_FINAL_v23 once you have been in Flow. He also noted being on a top podcast list among industry legends and beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Nikunj Kothari** automated most of his job away except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called frankly the hardest part of the job. He is still hunting for an insanely good multiplayer experience and asked builders to reply with a demo.

**Peter Steinberger** found inter-agent communication in the chat stream increasingly irritating and changed visibility in the OC harness to a single expandable line, betting others will follow. He also called the fight between CI and GitHub a close race on what slows him down, and said it is time to rethink how we work.

**Thariq** gave a candid look at prototype work on a game character, noting the graphics and feel are not high quality enough to play. A real game would need 3-4 variations of the character and 8-10 characters total, and he has tried 2-3 variations trying to fit a big jump into the control scheme. He said he would ideally work with a small group of 2-3 people.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** shines on in Skills, with skills rolling out globally in Gemini App as a faster way to automate repetitive tasks. The experiment will shut down on Nov 17, 2026, and the team thanked users for shaping the future of customizable AI.

**Amjad Masad** highlighted making and one-click publishing **Meta VR apps**.

**Aditya Agarwal** asked everyone at SPC what the maximalist version of their company is. His argument: start small and you will struggle to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent. This is the paradox of AI, it makes building easier but dramatically raises the floor on what it means to be a great startup.

**Matt Turck** noted SaaS companies seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Dan Shipper** said Every will be ready to vibe check, and asked Sundar Pichai to let them try the new model.

**Claude** announced Founder House coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says the Dots launch is the first thing that has given him back control of his mornings, and he frames the whole AI moment as a choice between an industrial revolution and a renaissance.

On the Every Podcast, Dan Shipper sat down with Sam Altman at DevDay, where OpenAI launched 22 new things, up from nine last year and four or five the year before. Altman admitted they cut several launches just to fit the 50 minute keynote, and that the pace is deliberate: if the tools make people more productive and people want more stuff, we better build more ourselves.

The centerpiece of the conversation was **Dots**, the personal agent Altman now trusts with his mornings. He described the old problem precisely: running a big company means something is always going wrong overnight, and checking his phone first thing polluted his brain and destroyed his deep work window. Now his dot, named **Dottie**, decides what he needs to think about and handles the rest. He told a story about a Slack message he spent 10 to 20 minutes failing to find with Codex, gave up on, and then the next day his dot had kept thinking about it overnight and found it, a screenshot he had forgotten was a screenshot.

Altman also described a live document that updates based on what he cares about, which has shifted from revenue and growth six months ago to safety, alignment, and security. He said seven people's dots working together can create something new that existed in all of our heads but was emergent, and that he would love a way to visualize what his dot thinks he cares about over years.

On the platform question, Altman drew the line between labs that hope there will only be one company in the world and OpenAI's bet on a renaissance of many companies. He acknowledged they have not figured out the App Store equivalent, but argued they have figured out how to help millions build significant new products. On the industrial revolution versus renaissance framing, he said the former was centered on machines and turned many people into cogs, while the latter was centered on people, and he gets sad hearing that AI will make humans into house cats. His hope is a wave of discovery, art, and company formation where people get the economic benefits and also get back control of their time.

He also touched on the **Decisions API**, the number one request on Luna being cheaper and faster, and the influence of Jev on that direction. On Codex native apps and bringing your ChatGPT subscription to third-party apps, he said he hates signing up for 100 subscriptions and that your subscription should work wherever you want. Asked what he is most excited about at the application layer, his answer was the ones we have not thought of, because there are way more good ideas outside the building than inside.

keywords: Gemini 4 | agents | platform
headline: Gemini 4 Lands as OpenAI Opens the Agent Platform
deck: Google ships a new flagship while OpenAI hands builders MCP servers, native apps, and subscription portability. The race shifts from raw models to who owns the deployment layer.
quote: I think people just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing.
quoteBy: Sam Altman · CEO, OpenAI

## 🧭 Daily Insights

**Core insight**: The conversation today is less about raw model capability and more about who owns the deployment layer. Google's Gemini 4 launch drew immediate reactions, but OpenAI's platform moves (MCP servers in ChatGPT, native apps, subscription portability) and Aaron Levie's thesis on AI as a services-and-deployment problem suggest the real battleground is integration, not intelligence. The trend forming: builders are being handed more surface area than ever, and the bottleneck is shifting from what you can build to what you should build.

## 𝕏 / TWITTER

**Josh Woodward** kept it short with a single post: "Gemini 4 Argon!" The VP of Google Labs and Gemini App signaled the flagship release with no elaboration, letting the model speak for itself.

**Peter Yang** reacted to the Gemini 4 launch with "Google cooked on Gemini 4!" but flagged the remaining gaps: he said Google still needs to be more competitive on its coding harness (**Antigravity**) and its personal agent (**Spark**). He also noted the whiplash of being called a shill in YouTube comments while being warmly received at DevDay.

**Thibault Sottiaux** announced that users can now build and deploy **MCP servers** directly through ChatGPT, with access controls to restrict or share them. He also reported that **GPT-6.1 Sol** is the most demanded model ever across API and subscriptions, with capacity being brought online to nearly double yesterday's serving speed. He shared a lighter moment about asking his dot to paint him, which found a recent photo online and improved its output.

**Nan Yu**, one week into his role at OpenAI, described the company's "absurdly high bias towards action" as its standout cultural trait. He gave examples: a casual feature discussion ending with a PR on your desk, or a lunch suggestion producing a calendar invite within five minutes. He acknowledged the downside of everything feeling "behind a hair trigger" but noted you cannot teach that energy once it is lost.

**Guillermo Rauch** argued that **Vercel AI Gateway** is the largest trustworthy datasource for global AI token flows, citing 400k+ paying customers, thousands of enterprises, and zero markup. He said Vercel turns down "free token" promos from companies with dubious **Zero Data Retention** claims, and that most providers "suck" so the goal is the best providers, not the most. He also pushed **Connect** as the fix for static credentials in agent building, framing connection as the final boss now that code is free.

**Aaron Levie** laid out a detailed thesis that the biggest opportunity is being the deployment layer for AI into the economy. He argued that AI agents deliver actual work augmentation, not just software tools, requiring legacy migration, data access updates, agent-connected software, reengineered workflows, **HITL** design, and maintained evals. He predicted new services firms and plays by industry, company size, and problem, calling it a great time to be an **FDE**.

**Garry Tan** shared a conversation with Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy at a Startup Industrial Base event in DC. Duffy described air traffic control running on six screens and paper flight charts, and pointed to **SMART** from Air Space Intelligence as a tool that predicts and could cut a 2.5 hour ground stop to 45 minutes. Duffy's response to criticism about handing air traffic to AI: "it's dumb AI, not smart AI," with controllers still making every call.

**Nikunj Kothari** said he has automated most of his job except sourcing and outbound emails, meeting founders in person, and writing pass notes by hand, which he called the hardest part. He also put out a call for anyone building an insanely good multiplayer experience to share a demo.

**Peter Steinberger** said inter-agent communication in the chat stream was becoming irritating, so he changed visibility in the **OC harness** to a single expandable line, predicting others will follow. He also noted the fierce fight between **CI** and **GitHub** over what slows him down, saying it is time to rethink how we work.

**Aditya Agarwal** said the question he keeps asking founders at SPC is "What is the maximalist version of this company?" He argued that starting small makes it hard to get funding, hire great people, and make a dent, calling it the paradox of AI: building is easier but the floor for a great startup has risen dramatically.

**Swyx** praised **Flow** for doing for hardware engineering what Git and GitHub did for software, aligning thousands of stakeholders on complex, irreversible pipelines from cars to rockets. He also expressed disbelief at ranking on a top podcast list among industry legends, beating MKBHD, despite doing nothing to optimize for Apple Podcasts.

**Thariq** shared progress on a game prototype, noting it is still prototype quality and that he would want a small group of 2-3 people to work on it. He said a real game would need 3-4 character variations and 8-10 characters total, and that he has tried 2-3 variations to fit a big jump into the control scheme.

**Google Labs** announced that **Opal** is shutting down on Nov 17, 2026, with its learnings living on in **Skills** in Gemini App. Skills are a faster way to automate repetitive tasks and save custom instructions in chat, rolling out globally.

**Amjad Masad** announced that users can make and one-click publish **Meta VR** apps on Replit.

**Matt Turck** posted a wry observation that SaaS companies are seeing Anthropic's ARR growth flatline.

**Dan Shipper** teased that Every will be ready to vibe check the new model and tagged Sundar Pichai asking to try it.

**Claude** announced **Claude Founder House** coming to SF Tech Week (Oct 6-8) and Stockholm (Oct 14), with talks, workshops, and office hours for founders, CEOs, and builders.

## 🎙 PODCASTS

**Takeaway**: Sam Altman says his dot, Dottie, gave him back his mornings and his deep work time, and that is the clearest signal yet that persistent agents are crossing from demo to daily utility.

In this episode of the Every Podcast (rebranded from AI & I), Dan Shipper sits down with Sam Altman at DevDay to talk about the 22 launches, the future of engineering, and how his personal dot changed his relationship with work. Altman opens by reflecting on the counterintuitive resilience of developer demand: "People just want so much more stuff. People's expectations are so high. And I think that's a wonderful thing." He notes that developers have never been able to do more, but there has never been a higher bar either.

The conversation turns to the renaissance versus industrial revolution framing. Altman argues the renaissance was centered on people while the industrial revolution was centered on machines, and that AI should push toward the former. He is openly dismissive of the "house cats" narrative where AI decides how to reward humans, saying that is certainly not what OpenAI thinks. Instead he hopes for a wave of scientific discovery, art, and company formation where people get both economic growth and control of their time.

The most concrete thread is Altman's personal use of Dots. He describes how checking his phone each morning used to pollute his brain and destroy his deep work period, but now he trusts his dot to triage what needs his attention. He shares a story where his dot noticed he had been searching Slack for something he could not find, kept thinking about it overnight, and found it the next day, a screenshot he had forgotten was an image. He also describes leaving a long voice note while drifting off to sleep and waking up to find not only the thing built but the next seven steps done.

On the platform strategy, Altman says OpenAI wants to enable a renaissance in company formation rather than being the one giant megacorp. He acknowledges they have not figured out the App Store equivalent, but believes they have figured out how to help millions build significant new products. He is most excited about the apps nobody at OpenAI can imagine today, and says the subscription portability feature exists because "it's your subscription, you should get to use wherever you want."
